# Hull

**Status:** in development; usable for testing single machines since 2026-10-22, on the terms in §12's *Adopting hull for testing*. Designed from August 2026, written from October.
**Name:** Hull — the hull is the vessel's isolation boundary. (Prior "Hull" CDP by hull.io was acquired by MessageBird in 2021 and discontinued; name considered safe for an open-source systems tool. Formerly working-titled "Crate".)

---

## 1. Motivation

Docker got the developer experience right and almost everything underneath it wrong for the long term:

- **Opaque formats.** Images are stacks of gzipped tarballs glued together by JSON manifests and a daemon-managed overlayfs graph. You cannot `ls` an image, diff two images with standard tools, dedupe files across images, or back up the store incrementally without Docker-specific tooling.
- **Root everywhere.** `dockerd` runs as root, owns a root-equivalent socket (`docker` group membership *is* root), and is a single point of failure for every container on the host.
- **Backend lock-in.** The isolation mechanism (shared-kernel namespaces) is baked into the product. When you need a real kernel boundary — untrusted code, kernel modules, a different kernel version — you leave the ecosystem entirely (Vagrant, raw QEMU, cloud VMs) and lose all your configuration.
- **Images where none are needed.** In the typical CI/deployment pipeline the image is pure ceremony: the build already produced the files, which get tarred into layers, pushed to a registry, pulled back down, and unpacked — five steps to move a directory. The image adds a second versioning system (with its own auth, GC policy, and layer-cache folklore) on top of the one you already have in VCS and CI artifacts.

Hull is a design for a runtime that fixes all four: a **files-first model** where a plain directory is the deployable unit and images are an optional module; a **rootless model with no privileged daemon** (the resident component is a per-user, unprivileged, socket-activated core — §2); and an **open, pluggable backend layer** where the same spec — and, crucially, the same files — runs under namespaces (`ns`), a hardware-virtualized machine (`vm`), full software emulation (`emu`), or any further engine that implements the driver interface, by changing one line.

Beneath all four sits one mechanism, Hull's core idea: **every image-shaped artifact — container image, filesystem image, disk, disc — is decomposed into plain structured files plus a verified binary patch** (§3.11). Opacity is never load-bearing; it is quarantined into patch files and measured.

### Goals

1. Every on-disk artifact is inspectable with standard Unix tools.
2. Zero root privileges required for install, build, or run (given `/dev/kvm` group access for the vm class). This holds fully for native engines; adapter engines inherit the wrapped runtime's posture (dockerd's root daemon, notably) and their matrix column says so.
3. One declarative spec; an **open set** of interchangeable backends — engine *classes* ns / vm / emu, implemented by concrete engines (docker, qemu, virtualbox, vmware first; §5.0) — with explicit, plan-time-checked capability differences — never silent behavior drift — plus namespaced per-backend sections for tuning that is legitimately backend-specific.
4. Full interoperability with the OCI ecosystem at the border (pull/push any registry image).
5. Migration paths from Dockerfiles and compose files.
6. **Files first.** A plain directory is the *primary* machine source on every backend: unpack files, point the spec at the directory, run. The image store is an optional module for snapshots/signing/dedup — Hull is fully functional with it absent, and a CI/deployment pipeline never needs to touch an image, a registry, or a layer.
7. **Identical ingress.** SSH and network access to a machine work exactly the same way on every backend — same name, same address, same host key. Switching backends must never break a shell, a mount, or an IDE connection recipe.

### Non-goals (v1)

- Being a Kubernetes CRI runtime (the design keeps the door open; v1 does not walk through it).
- Windows containers.
- Swarm-style multi-host orchestration.
- Reimplementing low-level runtimes: Hull orchestrates Docker, QEMU, VirtualBox, VMware, and crun; it does not replace them.

---

## 2. Architecture overview

```
        frontends: CLI · TUI · Qt/Widgets GUI
                            │  (shared encrypted protocol — per project guidelines)
              hull core (C; per-user, unprivileged, socket-activated)
                            │
        ┌───────────────────┼──────────────────────┐
        │                   │                      │
   spec compiler       sources               engine plugins (by class)
   (hull.conf →       (dir: trees, or       ┌───────────────────────┐
    ExecutionPlan)      CAS image store      │ ns:  docker → native  │
        │               + composefs)         │ vm:  qemu, virtualbox,│
        └── capability      │                │      vmware           │
            matrix check    │                │ emu: qemu (tcg)       │
                            │                │ …reserved slots       │
                            │                └───────────────────────┘
                            │                        │
                     OCI border tools          guest agent
                     (pull/push/convert)       (vsock, VM
                                                backends only)
```

Lifecycle of `hull run`:

1. Parse `hull.conf` → abstract **MachineSpec**.
2. Resolve the source: a `dir:` path is used as-is (§3.7); an image ref is resolved against the local store (pull + convert from OCI if absent).
3. **Plan:** compile MachineSpec + chosen backend into an ExecutionPlan; run every spec field through the capability matrix; refuse with a precise error if any field can't be honored on this backend.
4. **Execute:** hand the plan to the engine. Machines run as children of the per-user core (or of a generated systemd user unit) — there is no root daemon anywhere; the core is an unprivileged service belonging to the invoking user, and the CLI, TUI, and Qt GUI are peer frontends speaking the shared encrypted protocol to it (local or remote). *(The exact frontend/core split is finalized against the project guidelines — see Handoff notes.)*

The first-wave engines (docker, qemu, virtualbox, vmware — §5.0) ship with the core, but the driver interface is a stable plugin API: further engines — DOSBox, Wine, user-mode Linux, wasm runtimes — register themselves together with a capability declaration and become `(backend …)` values like any other.

---

## 3. Image store (deep dive) — an *optional module*

Read this section knowing its place: **nothing in Hull requires the store.** Dir sources (§3.7) are the primary mode and work with the store code entirely absent — the store exists for those who want snapshots, dedup across many machines, signing, and OCI interop. §3.8 describes the image-free deployment workflow that is Hull's main use case.

### 3.1 On-disk layout

Everything lives under `~/.local/share/hull/` (XDG-compliant, overridable):

```
store/
  objects/sha256/ab/abcdef…        # content-addressed regular files, mode 0444
  manifests/sha256/…               # image manifests (canonical, A.1)
  refs/
    registry.example.com/webapp/2.1 → ../manifests/sha256/…   # symlinks; tags are just symlinks
  mounts/                          # composefs images generated from manifests (cache, disposable)
machines/
  <machine-id>/
    spec.resolved.conf             # frozen copy of the spec at creation time
    upper/                         # writable layer (plain directory)
    state.conf                     # pid/cid, backend, ports, timestamps
```

Key properties:

- **`objects/` is a plain CAS.** One copy of each unique file across *all* images and *all* snapshots, hard-link-friendly, `rsync`-able, `du`-honest. Executable bit is the only mode stored on the object itself; full metadata lives in manifests.
- **A manifest is the image.** It's a small table mapping `path → (object hash, mode, uid/gid mapping class, xattrs, symlink target | directory | hardlink group)`. Pulling an image whose files you mostly have transfers only the missing objects — dedup is global and automatic, not layer-accidental.
- **Tags are symlinks.** `hull tag`, `hull images`, and rollbacks are filesystem operations you can audit with `ls -l`.
- **No layer stack at rest.** Layers are a *transport and build-cache* concept, not a storage concept. Two images that share 95% of files share 95% of objects regardless of how their Dockerfile-era layers were arranged.

**Written 2026-10-21, the first of it: `store/store.c`, behind `hull commit DIR REF`, `hull materialize REF DIR`, `hull images`, and the store's part of `hull gc`.** The layout is the one above, at `$XDG_DATA_HOME/hull/store`; `machines/` and `mounts/` are not made, since nothing uses them yet.
- **A commit** walks the directory, puts each regular file into `objects/` by its sha256, written under `tmp/` and linked in, so an object is whole or absent, 0444 or 0555 as its file was executable, and writes Appendix A's canonical manifest by the same rule, named by its own sha256, and the ref as a symlink to it, replaced by a rename. A copy of the tree, or the tree with its mtimes moved, is the same manifest.
- **Decided here, where Appendix A is open, and each the holder's to change:**
  - **owner:** a file the user owns is `(owner root)`, since that is what a dir source's files are inside its machine (§4.8, §4.11); any other is the explicit `((gid n) (uid n))`. Nothing is yet `(owner user)`.
  - **hardlinks** are committed as one `reg` entry per path, sharing their object. **Open, the holder's to decide:** A.2's `hardlink` kind has a `group` and no `object`, and `group` belongs to `kind hardlink` only, so no entry says which member's content a group has; a set is either all `hardlink` with nowhere for the content, or one `reg` that the group cannot name. Until that is settled the store keeps the content and loses the inode's sharing, and materializing gives each path a file of its own.
  - **stats:** `file_count` counts the entries, `object_count` the distinct objects, and `total_size` the regular files' sizes, each counted once per path. A.2's example has two entries and a `file_count` of 3, which fits none of these.
  - **paths** must be UTF-8; NFC is not checked. A socket is left out and said to be; so is a path that is not UTF-8.
- **Materializing** checks the manifest against its name and every object against its own as it copies. It makes each path with its parent opened by `openat2` beneath the target and through no symlink, so a manifest whose symlink leads out cannot write through it, and a path with `..` is refused before that. A target must be absent or empty. Directories are made 0700 and given their modes last, deepest first. **The tree's own root has no entry in the manifest** — a commit records the tree's contents, not its root — so it is given 0755 last of all, a directory anyone may enter: a non-root uid in a build step cannot traverse a 0700 root, `EACCES` on the `execve` of a 0755 binary beneath it, which is how apt's download sandbox and any maintainer script that drops privilege failed in a step until 2026-10-25. Found by netcfgd, who disproved ownership (a root-owned `partial` pre-created as `_apt` gave the same warning) and the sandbox being apt's own (`su -s /bin/cat _apt` and `setpriv` both `EACCES` on exec) by reading the path: `/` 0700 while everything beneath had its mode. `hull_store_checkout` had set 0755 for a run already; `materialize_locked` now does it for every caller, so `hull build` steps and `hull materialize` get it too. Preserving a non-0755 root, were an image ever to ship one, would mean recording the root in the commit, which it does not; 0755 is the root every image has. Every path is given the user's own group, and a file its mode again after: one made in a setgid directory takes that directory's group, which a machine's user namespace, mapping only the user's group, cannot write under, and a chown clears a file's setuid and setgid bits. Found 2026-10-21, when a machine on a checkout under the setgid build tree failed a write with EOVERFLOW. What the user cannot make rootless is said and left: a device, an explicit owner, which becomes the user's, and an xattr the kernel refuses, as `security.*` is.
- **gc** takes the store's lock exclusively, where a commit or a materialize takes it shared, so it cannot take an object between its write and the ref that reaches it. A ref whose manifest cannot be read stops it rather than let it take that manifest's objects. It also takes the checkouts of the manifests no ref reaches, and empties `tmp/`, each tree with its directories made writable first, no symlink followed. A checkout a machine still runs on goes too, since the store does not know its machines.
- **A bare source the store holds runs that tree, written 2026-10-21.** J.1 says a bare string is an image ref in the store or a registry. Before the file is read as a spec, the frontend looks each machine's bare source up as a ref or `sha256:HEX`, and where the store holds it, writes the source in the file's text as `"dir:` and the tree's checkout `"`, and `(mutable overlay)` after it where the machine names no mutability. So the registry, the matrix and the plan judge the dir source the engine is given, and every engine that takes one runs it: chroot in `test/cli_test.sh`, docker and qemu by hand the same day. `(mutable direct)` is refused, since nothing writes a stored tree. A ref the store does not hold is left an image ref, docker's. A line's columns after its source move by the difference in length in what a plan reports, since the text moved; its lines do not.
  - **A checkout** is `checkouts/sha256/HEX`, materialized once under `tmp/` and renamed in, under the store's shared lock, so it is the tree or absent and gc cannot take it half made; one made meanwhile by another run wins, and this one's is removed. Its root is 0755, as a dir source's would be. `hull run` makes it; `hull plan` and `hull check` only name it.
  - **Measured** in `test/cli_test.sh`: a plan naming the checkout and making none; a run writing in the machine, the checkout and the committed tree unchanged after; a second run on the same checkout; `(mutable direct)` refused; a ref the store does not hold planned on docker; and gc keeping the checkout while its ref is there and taking it after. In `test/store_test.sh`: a tree materialized under a setgid directory of another group, every path the user's group; a setuid and setgid file materialized with its mode; and a read-only tree in `tmp/` taken by gc.
  - **Sabotage,** baseline first, through `make test` in scratch copies: no rewrite, no overlay added, `(mutable direct)` taken, a run making no checkout, a plan making one, gc keeping every checkout or taking every one, the group not given, a file's mode not given again after it, and a tree's directories not made writable before it is removed each failed a named case. Three of those had no case to fail until one was written for it: gc taking a checkout a ref reaches, the mode after the chown, and the read-only tree. The last also left the test's own directory where rm could not remove it, so both suites now make their trees writable before removing them, and the store suite fails when its directory is left. **The tree root left 0700 rather than 0755** is caught by the store suite too, which stats the materialized root: sabotaged in place, baseline first, `fchmod(root, 0700)` makes exactly that one of 71 cases fail.
- **`hull diff A B`, written 2026-10-21,** says what differs from one tree to another, each named by a ref or by `sha256:HEX`, as `materialize` now also takes; no ref may begin `sha256:`. Both manifests are in path order, so one walk of the two says each path once, in that order: `+ path` come, `- path` gone, and `~ path: what` changed, where what is the fields, as `kind reg -> dir`, `mode 0644 -> 0755`, `owner root -> 1000:1000`, `content` for an object or a size, `target a -> b`, `device`, `xattrs`, and `other` for a field no manifest of this version writes. A changed kind is said with the mode and owner alone, since the other fields belong to the kinds. The tree's own arch, where it differs, is a line of its own. A path's bytes below a space, DEL and the backslash are printed as `\` and three octal digits, so a newline in a name cannot begin a line. It exits as diff(1) does, 0 for the same, 1 for different, 2 for trouble, and refuses, with 2, a manifest whose entries are not in path order, which none of its writer's is, rather than say a change it could not have.
  - **Measured** in `test/store_test.sh`: nothing between a tree and its copy, nor between a tree and the tree materialized from it and committed again, the round trip being the same manifest; a manifest named by its hash the same as by its ref; every change above but owner and device, which rootless cannot make, said exactly, in path order, one name with a newline in it and one with a quote and a backslash, and 1; an arch alone; a ref that is not, a hash that is not one, a hash with no manifest, and a manifest out of order refused with 2; and a commit to a ref named as a hash refused.
  - **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies of the store suite: the order unchecked, a kind said with the fields of its kinds, the mode unsaid, a newline printed as it is, the arch uncompared, every path said changed, come and gone swapped, no `sha256:` names, a ref named as a hash taken, the xattrs uncompared and the target uncompared each failed a named case.
- **`hull tag REF NEW`, `hull tag -d REF` and `hull manifest show REF`, written 2026-10-21.** A tag is a second ref to the manifest a ref or `sha256:HEX` names, which the store must hold; a ref already there is replaced, as docker's tag does, and it is set under the shared lock, so gc cannot take the manifest between its read and the ref. `-d` removes a ref and leaves its manifest for gc.
  - **The view** is A.1's: the manifest and its entries a line per element, the rest of an entry on its line, so two views diff by entry. An atom is a bare token where its bytes are letters, digits and `_-.:/+@`, a string where it is other text, with `\\`, `\"`, `\n` and `\t` escaped, and `#hex#` where it is not UTF-8 text or holds another control byte; `path` and `target` are strings always, as A.2 has them. A mode is the canonical decimal, as A.1 says, not octal. `hull manifest compile`, the view's way back into the store, is not written.
  - **Measured** in `test/store_test.sh`: the view of a tree with a name holding a newline, one holding a quote and a backslash, and an xattr that is not text, read back by a reader of the test's own, apart from hull's, to the canonical bytes whose hash is the manifest's name; an entry a line; a path a string; a tag by ref, by hash and over a ref; `-d`, its manifest kept; and a missing ref, a name that is not one, a ref named as a hash and a hash with no manifest refused.
  - **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies of the store suite: no `#hex#`, a quote unescaped, a path as a token, the entries on one line, a tag of a hash with no manifest, and `-d` keeping the ref each failed a named case. No `#hex#` first survived, binary bytes inside a string reading back the same, so the view is now required to be printable too.
- **Not written yet:** commit from a machine, composefs mounting, and fs-verity; pull and push are §3.5's.
- **Measured** in `test/store_test.sh`, which `make test` runs: a tree of every kind committed, a socket left out; the manifest and each of its 15 entries verified against `manifest/manifest.situ` by `situc`, apart from the code that wrote them, and the entries in bytewise order of their paths, read apart too; a copy with its mtimes moved the same manifest, a change a new one and one new object; objects 0444 and 0555; the tree materialized as it was, its hardlink as two files; a target not empty, a ref not there, a name not one, and a directory not there refused; an object and a manifest each altered by a byte refused; a crafted manifest whose symlink leads out refused at the entry through it, and one with `..` refused, nothing written outside; and gc removing the three manifests and the one object no ref reaches and keeping the rest, the tree materializing whole after it. The test's `hull gc` reaches a core of its own with no daemon, so nothing of the account's is touched.
- **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies: leaving the entries unsorted, an object's hash or a manifest's unchecked, `openat2`'s resolve flags off, `..` allowed, gc taking an object a ref reaches or leaving one none does, xattrs dropped, and directory modes not set each failed the store suite. The unsorted entries first survived it: a copy of a tree reads back in the order it was made, and situc checks each entry's shape, not their order. Two of the ten whole-suite runs also failed in the cli suite, on the core's idle time (§4.10's open items), which `make test` runs first, so those two were run again with the store suite alone.

### 3.2 Mounting

- **ns backend:** the manifest is compiled (once, cached in `mounts/`) into a **composefs/EROFS** image — standard, in-kernel, mountable read-only with fs-verity integrity if enabled — and mounted with the machine's `upper/` directory as an overlayfs upper layer. No custom graph driver, no daemon bookkeeping: unmount + delete the directory and the machine is gone.
- **vm backend:** the *same* composefs mount is exported into the guest over **virtiofs**. Container and VM literally see the same bytes; there is no image conversion step when switching backends.
- **emu backend:** identical to vm — QEMU TCG serves virtiofs exactly the same way; no conversion when switching. (Only engines without virtiofs — virtualbox, vmware, hyperv — need a materialized disk image: the §3.9 block projection, cached by manifest hash and shared across those engines rather than reimplemented per engine.)

### 3.3 Writable layer & snapshots

The machine's `upper/` is a plain directory — the overlayfs upper under `bind`/`passthrough` projections. Under a `block` projection (§3.9) writes land in a qcow2 overlay instead and reach the source tree only via declared `writeback`; `hull commit` on such a machine walks the overlay's filesystem rather than `upper/`. In the common case, `hull commit` walks `upper/`, hashes new files into `objects/`, and emits a new manifest — a snapshot is just another manifest, ~kilobytes. `hull diff machineA imageB` is a manifest comparison: exact, fast, and printable as a normal file list.

### 3.4 Garbage collection

`hull gc` computes reachability: refs → manifests → objects; everything unreachable is deleted. Because objects are immutable and mode-0444, GC is safe to run concurrently with pulls (writes land under a tempname and are hard-linked in atomically). No "dangling layer" mystery accumulation.

### 3.5 OCI interop (the border)

**Decided 2026-10-21, by the holder: `hull pull` links OpenSSL and zlib.** libssl and libcrypto give verified TLS to a registry and zlib unpacks the layers, both on every Linux host; hull owns the rest, a small HTTP/1.1 client, a JSON reader for the registry's manifests, and the tar applier with its whiteouts. Only the frontend links them; the agent stays static and untouched. Rejected: running curl and gzip, which leaves errors and streaming to a subprocess and needs both installed; and pulling through the docker daemon, which needs docker running, against the rootless aim.

**`hull pull IMAGE [REF]`, written 2026-10-21** (`oci/pull.c`, `oci/https.c`, `oci/json.c`, `store/unpack.c`). The image is named as docker names one, `[REGISTRY/]REPOSITORY[:TAG][@sha256:HEX]`, docker.io and `library/` where none is given, `latest` where no tag is; it is committed as REF, or as the name as given, so that `(source "alpine:3.20")` runs what `hull pull alpine:3.20` pulled, as §3.1's bare source does.
- **The registry** is reached by HTTPS only, OpenSSL verifying the peer against the host's trust store, which `SSL_CERT_FILE` may name, and its name or address against the URL's, TLS 1.2 at least. A 401 with a Bearer challenge is answered with a token from its realm, for the scope it names, once, asked for with the user's credentials where there are any and anonymously where not; a Basic challenge is answered with the credentials themselves, sent to the registry's own host alone. A redirect is followed five deep, and the token goes only to the registry's own host, not to wherever its blobs are served from. One GET is one connection, its body framed by its length, by chunks, or by a TLS close, and refused when it ends short.
- **What is checked:** a manifest fetched by digest is that digest; the config and every layer are their digests, hashed as they come, and a layer is its declared size. A manifest fetched by tag is what TLS brought, and its digest is said. An index is narrowed to `linux` on the host's arch, by OCI's name for it, `amd64` or `arm64`; a variant is not read. The config must be for that arch.
- **Layers** are gzip or plain tar; zstd and foreign layers are refused by name. Each is held under the store's `tmp/`, the store's lock shared from the first write there to the commit, so that gc waits, and applied in order into a tree beside it: every parent opened with `openat2` and `RESOLVE_IN_ROOT`, so that a layer's symlinked directory, `/lib` to `usr/lib`, is followed as the tree's own and a symlink out of it leads only inside it; a path with `..` refused; a whiteout removing what a lower layer made, an opaque one what lower layers made in its directory; ustar, GNU long names and pax headers read, a pax `SCHILY.xattr.user.*` set and any other said and left; every directory 0700 until the last layer is in, then its own mode, deepest first, so that a read-only directory can be written by a later layer; and a device said and left out.
- **What is lost, and the holder's to change:** owners. Every path is the user's, so it is committed as `(owner root)`, and a file an image gives to uid 101 is the user's like the rest; the tar's ids are not yet carried into the manifest. Not read either: zstd, a proxy, and plain HTTP.
- **Credentials, since 2026-10-21,** are the user's own, read where podman and docker keep them and in that order, the first file holding them for the registry taken: `$REGISTRY_AUTH_FILE`, `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/containers/auth.json`, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/containers/auth.json` (`~/.config`), and `$DOCKER_CONFIG/config.json` (`~/.docker`). An `auths` key names the registry by its host, its host and port, or a URL of it, and Docker Hub by any of the names it is filed under, `https://index.docker.io/v1/` among them; its `auth` is the base64 of `user:password`, sent as it is and never printed. They go to a Bearer challenge's realm when the token is asked for, as docker sends them, the realm being the registry's to name, and to the registry itself on a Basic challenge, and to no host a redirect names: a header of the caller's carrying `Authorization` is dropped once a redirect leaves the host it was meant for. A credential helper, `credsStore` or `credHelpers`, is named and not run, since it is a program; an `identitytoken` is not read. Credentials a registry refuses are said to be refused; none found is said too.
  - **Measured** in `test/pull_test.sh`, whose home is its own so that the account's files are not read: the test registry's `test/private`, whose token is given only for its credentials, refused with none, pulled with them by host and by a URL of it, refused with wrong ones, and pulled with podman's file read before docker's wrong one; `test/basic`, which asks for Basic, pulled with them and refused without; `test/hopped`, whose realm redirects to `localhost`, which refuses any request carrying credentials, pulled; a helper named; a push to a private repository; and the password and its base64 printed nowhere, and the blob host, which refuses any `Authorization`, sent none.
  - **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies: credentials kept across a redirect to another host, Basic sent to every host, URL keys unread, docker's file read before podman's, a Basic challenge refused, no credentials sent to the realm, and the helper unsaid each failed a named case. The redirected realm had no case to fail until `test/hopped` was written for it.
- **The config is A.4's runtime, and the pull is A.5's provenance, since 2026-10-21.** `Cmd`, `Entrypoint`, `Env`, `ExposedPorts`, `StopSignal`, `User`, `Volumes` and `WorkingDir` become `(runtime …)` in canonical order: the words as given, `env` sorted by name with a name given twice its last value, as docker takes it, the ports and volumes sorted, and the workdir `/` where the config names none, since the schema requires one. `(provenance (builder "hull VERSION") (oci (digests …) (registry …)))` follows A.5: the digests in the order fetched, index, manifest, config and each layer, and the registry as the image's name names it, `docker.io/library/alpine`. Provenance is part of the manifest's hash, so a pull by digest, which fetches no index, is another manifest than a pull by tag, its entries and runtime the same. `hull diff` says a runtime that differs by its keys, and a provenance that differs.
- **`hull push REF IMAGE`, written 2026-10-21** (`oci/push.c`), publishes a stored tree as a single-layer image, as the bullet above says a push re-serializes. The layer is the tar of the manifest, written by the store from the manifest and its objects, nothing materialized: entries in the manifest's order, each directory before what it holds, every mtime 0, `(owner root)` as 0:0 and explicit ids as themselves, xattrs as pax `SCHILY.xattr` records, binary as they are, a device with its numbers, and each object checked against its name as it streams; an owner of class `user`, which has no number in an image, is refused, though nothing writes one yet. It goes through zlib's gzip, whose header carries no time, so one tree is one layer digest each time it is pushed. The config is the manifest's arch and its A.4 runtime in OCI's names, its `diff_ids` the tar's hash; a tree with no runtime pushes an empty `config`. Each blob is asked for with a HEAD and sent only where the registry lacks it, a POST opening the upload and a PUT to its Location closing it with the digest; the manifest is PUT under the tag last, and the registry's digest for it, where it gives one, must be ours. A push names a tag, not a digest. The layer waits in an `O_TMPFILE` under the store's `tmp/`, with no name for gc to take. The registry's session, the token and the redirects, is `oci/remote.c`, which pull and push share, a request now taking a method and a body (`oci/https.c`), and a 307 or 308 keeping both. `engine/tree.c` writes each tar header from an entry record, which the store's layer and the docker adapter's import both use, the import's bytes unchanged.
  - **Not done:** chunked uploads, one PUT carrying the whole layer; a layer split by size, which §3.5 allows; and the original layering of a pulled image, which a push does not keep.
  - **Measured** in `test/pull_test.sh`, against the test's registry, which takes uploads, refuses a blob that is not its digest and a manifest naming a blob not there, and logs each blob it takes: the pulled image pushed, its layer read by Python's `gzip` and `tarfile`, apart from hull, every member root's at time 0, the xattr there, and the config's `diff_id` the tar's hash; the image pulled back, `hull diff` saying the provenance alone; pushed again, the same digest and no blob sent; a tree with no runtime pushed and pulled back with the workdir a pull gives; and an object altered by a byte, a push by digest, a ref not there, and an untrusted CA refused. The docker suite passes on the refactored tar writer.
  - **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies: the layer's owner the user, a time given, xattrs left out, every blob sent again, the diff_id the blob's hash, a config without the runtime, objects unchecked, a push to a digest taken, env written as names alone, and a binary pax record cut a byte short each failed a named case.
- **A machine on a stored tree takes its runtime** where it says nothing of its own: its command, the entrypoint and then the command, as docker runs them; its workdir; and each env name it does not set, so its own win, as J.1 says. They are written into the file's text with the source, so `hull plan --resolved` shows them. The image's user is said and not taken, since J.1 has no key for it, and the machine runs as its root; the ports and volumes are advisory. A bare command is searched for in the machine's `PATH` on every engine since the same day, so `python:3.12-alpine`'s `(cmd python3)` runs on chroot with no command of the machine's own.
  - **Measured** in `test/pull_test.sh`: a config's defaults as the exact runtime, `GREETING` given twice its last, the ports out of order sorted; a config with none as `(runtime (cmd /probe) (workdir /))`; the provenance's six digests and registry; the pulled manifest verified against `manifest/manifest.situ` by `situc`; a machine naming nothing running the image's entrypoint and command in its workdir with its env, told its user is not taken; a machine's own workdir and env winning; a diff against the tree committed again saying the runtime and the provenance alone, and one against a pull by digest the provenance alone. In scratch copies, no runtime written, the env not deduplicated, the names unsorted, no workdir default, no index digest, the machine's env lost, no command given, no workdir given, the command before the entrypoint, the user unsaid and a diff blind to the runtime each failed a named case.
- **Measured by hand** against Docker Hub: `alpine:3.20`, one layer, 517 entries, and `python:3.12-alpine`, four, 3347 entries and 2041 new objects of 2088, each pulled and run on chroot, alpine's shell and `apk`, python's interpreter with its `ssl` and `sqlite3`.
- **Measured** in `test/pull_test.sh`, which `make test` runs, against `test/registry.py`, a registry of the test's own over TLS with a CA the test makes, so nothing reaches the network: the token, asked for and given; each blob redirected to `localhost`, which refuses a request carrying the token; a layer in chunks; an index with another arch's manifest first; three layers, the last not compressed; and in the tree after, a file a later layer replaced and its hardlink keeping the old content, a whiteout, an opaque directory, writes through a symlinked directory and through a symlink to `/`, landing in the tree, a read-only directory written by a later layer and given its mode after, a pax long name, an xattr, and no device; the image run on chroot, its name its source; a second pull with nothing new, and one by digest giving the same tree. Refused, nothing committed and nothing left in `tmp/`: a layer that is not its digest, a symlink out written through, nothing written where it led, a `..`, zstd, an index with no platform for the arch, saying those it has, a config for another arch, a layer longer than its size, a manifest that is not its digest, a tag not there, a peer no CA trusts, a certificate for another name, and a repository that is not one. `test/json_test.c` holds 40 cases of the reader, each refusal a byte from a document it takes. fmake builds it all, finding OpenSSL and zlib itself, and the pull suite passes on its `hull` too.
  - **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies through `make test`: a layer, a manifest or a size unchecked, the token sent to any host or never asked for, chunks read as a body, the peer's name unverified, an index's first platform taken, the config's arch unchecked, parents resolved outside the tree, `..` taken, a whiteout or an opaque one ignored, an opaque one taking its own layer's paths, directory modes never given or given at once, a pax path ignored, xattrs ignored, and the JSON reader taking a trailing comma or pairing no surrogates, each failed a named case. Two first survived. Turning off OpenSSL's peer verification alone did, because the connection is also refused unless the verify result is good, so either check holds it; with both off, the untrusted CA's case fails. And an opaque whiteout taking its own layer's paths did, the test layer having none before its marker; it has one now.

- `hull pull`: fetch OCI image, apply layers in order *in memory of a scratch dir*, hash resulting files into the CAS, emit a manifest. Original OCI digests are recorded in the manifest's provenance block for supply-chain audit.
- `hull push`: re-serialize a manifest into a single-layer (or chunked-by-size) OCI image. Round-tripping is lossless for file content and metadata; by default it does not preserve the original layer structure (documented, and irrelevant to registries). Workflows that need **digest-exact** round-trip get it via the §3.11 patch model applied to OCI: a layering sidecar records layer boundaries and compression parameters, and a patch covers whatever deterministic recompression can't reproduce — the original layer digests reconstruct verifiably.
- Signature story: fs-verity digests per object + a signed manifest gives per-file integrity that OCI's per-layer signing can't express; cosign-compatible signing of the OCI export keeps the ecosystem happy.

### 3.6 Builds

`hull build` consumes a `(build …)` form (in the same file or in `hull.build.conf`):

```scheme
(build
  (base "docker.io/library/debian:13-slim")
  (steps
    (step
      (name "deps")
      (run "apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends python3")
      (inputs))                 ; cache key: base manifest + this command only
    (step
      (name "app")
      (copy (path (from "./src") (to "/app")))
      (run "pip install -e /app")
      (inputs "./src"))))       ; cache key includes a content hash of ./src
```

Each step runs in a throwaway machine (any backend; ns by default) and commits to a manifest. The cache key of a step is `hash(parent manifest, step definition, content hash of declared inputs)` — caching is **correct by construction**, not by Dockerfile line-order heuristics. Undeclared inputs simply aren't visible to the step (the build mount namespace only maps declared paths), which is what makes the cache sound rather than hopeful.

**`hull build [-f FILE] REF`, written 2026-10-22,** the holder having chosen to copy, run direct and commit each step. The base is a stored ref or `sha256:HEX`, a `dir:` path committed as it is, or an image ref the store has not, pulled first. Each step in order:
- **Its key** is the sha256 of the parent's manifest, the step's form in canonical bytes, and each input's digest, its `inputs` and its copies' `from`s, a directory's being its manifest, committed so that its objects are in the store, a file's its mode and bytes; with `(network true)`, the period of its `refresh` it falls in, `"7d"` by default, so a step with a network is run again once a period has passed. A step whose key the store holds, as the ref `build/cache/KEY`, is not run.
- **Its tree** is its parent materialized under the store's `tmp/`, which a hold on the store's lock keeps from gc for the whole build, its copies put in: a directory's committed tree, the directory given its source's mode, or a file, replacing what is there. Each copy's path is opened in the tree with `openat2` and `RESOLVE_IN_ROOT`, so a symlink of the parent's, `/lib` to `/usr/lib`, is followed as the tree's own, a path with `..` is refused, and one through a symlink to where the tree has nothing is refused rather than made.
- **Its run line** is `/bin/sh -c LINE`, in a machine the frontend runs as `hull run` runs one: `(source "dir:TREE") (mutable direct)`, on chroot for `ns` or no backend and on the one named otherwise; `(network none)` unless it says `(network true)`, which is the host's on chroot and the engine's own elsewhere; the parent's runtime workdir, or `/`; and the parent's runtime env, then `PATH` where it gives none, and `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` 0. A line that exits other than 0 fails the build, and nothing is tagged.
- **Its commit** drops what `/tmp` holds and its `discard` paths, and carries the parent's runtime, and A.5's provenance, `(build (inputs …) (parent …) (step …))` and the builder; its manifest is the cache's ref. The last step's is tagged REF.
- **A step's env and workdir, and a build's runtime, since 2026-10-22,** J.6's `step.env`, `step.workdir` and `build.runtime`, added for the Dockerfile importer the holder chose next, whose ENV, WORKDIR, CMD and ENTRYPOINT had nowhere to go. A step's env names replace its parent's runtime's for its run, its workdir is made in the tree where missing, and both are part of its form and so of its key. The build's runtime is laid over the last tree's as a manifest of the same entries and provenance with another runtime (`hull_store_with_runtime()`), not a step: nothing runs, and nothing is cached. Measured in `test/build_test.sh`: a step's env and workdir, the workdir made and written in; a build's runtime exactly as given, and a machine on the image running its command in its workdir; one laid over a base that has one, env merged, `(cmd)` clearing, ports joined and the workdir kept, the manifest still the schema's by `situc`. In scratch copies, a step's env lost, its workdir lost or not made, the build's runtime not laid, the base's env lost, the ports not joined, and an empty `(cmd)` kept each failed a named case; the base's env first survived, the build overriding the one name the base had, and the base now has one the build does not.
- **`hull build --check`, written the same day,** is Appendix E's reproducibility test: every step run twice from the base, the cache read by neither pass, and each step's two manifests compared. The first that differs is named, with both digests and `hull diff`'s lines between them, a step with a network said to be one Appendix E does not expect to reproduce, and the build exits 1 with nothing tagged; one whose every step matched says so and is tagged. The probe gained `stamp PATH`, the time to the nanosecond, as the step the suite's check must catch. In scratch copies, the cache read in a check, one pass alone, and the passes not compared each failed a named case.
- **Where it parts from Appendix E,** and the holder's to change: the parent's tree is a writable copy, not a read-only composefs mount with an overlay, and a copy is put in the tree, not bind-mounted from a staging snapshot, being part of what the step commits, as a `COPY` is. `/proc` and `/dev` are what the chroot engine gives a machine, `/dev` its own since 2026-10-23 (§4.8). a step on a vm is not tried, and nor is a step reading a pulled base's runtime env and workdir, no base in the suite having one. The cache's refs, `build/cache/…`, are listed by `hull images` with the rest.
- **Measured** in `test/build_test.sh`, which `make test` runs, on chroot, the probe standing for `/bin/sh` as `probe STEP …`: three steps, writing, copying a directory and a file, and dialling the host with and without a network; the tree after, the writes and copies in it, the copied directory its source's mode, `/tmp` emptied and kept, a discard dropped; the built manifest verified against `manifest/manifest.situ` by `situc`, its provenance the step's; built again with the pure steps cached, and past the refresh period the network step alone run again; an input changed, its step and those after run, and a step's line changed, it and those after; `--check` of the suite's build, every step run twice uncached and reproducible, and of one that stamps the time, its stamping step named, its `~ stamp: content` said, nothing tagged, and its pure step not named; a base the store holds; a failing step tagging nothing; a copy with `..` refused; a copy through the base's symlink to `/usr/lib` landing in the tree's, and one through its symlink to a host directory refused, nothing written there; and nothing left in `tmp/`.
  - **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies: the inputs, the step and the parent each left out of the key, every step networked, no refresh period, `/tmp` kept, a discard kept, copies resolved on the host, a copied directory's mode unset, a failing step tagged, no provenance, and the scratch left each failed a named case; the parent and the step's own line had no case to fail until one was written for them.

### 3.7 Directory sources — the primary mode

`(source "dir:./rootfs")` runs a plain directory as the machine's root. This is Hull's default way of working, not an escape hatch: the store, manifests, and pulls are bypassed entirely; the directory *is* the machine's filesystem, on every backend, with no conversion step: ns idmap-bind-mounts it as rootfs, and vm/emu export the same directory as the rootfs virtiofs share.

Write semantics are per machine:

- `(mutable direct)` (the default for dir sources): writes land in the directory itself. Edit a config file on the host, restart the service in the guest — or the reverse. `git status` your rootfs if you like; the files are the truth. (This describes `bind` and `passthrough` projections; a block projection cannot write through to the directory, so the matrix rejects `direct` there unless `writeback` is declared — §3.9.)
- `(mutable overlay)`: the directory stays pristine; writes go to the machine's `upper/` exactly as with image sources.

Ownership: a single-uid tree — the common case for a service rootfs — needs nothing special: the user's files are root's in a machine, on chroot by its user namespace and on vm and emu by qemu's (§4.11). **A tree of many owners keeps them in the store's manifest, given back when a machine starts, as §7 decided 2026-10-22 and 2026-10-23,** not as a `user.hull.owner` xattr on each file, which this paragraph once said: an idmapped mount maps ranges and not a file's own attribute, so presenting such xattrs would need a FUSE layer of its own. The holder chose the manifest's way on 2026-10-23.

**Layers and a kept upper, designed 2026-10-23 at the holder's asking, built on chroot the same day:** a read-only template with more read-only layers over it, and the machine's writes kept in a directory of the user's that may hold files of its own, as unionfs did and overlayfs, its successor in the kernel, does.

```scheme
(machine
  (source "dir:./template")              ; the lowest layer, read-only
  (layers "dir:./site" "dir:./extra")    ; each above the last, read-only
  (mutable overlay)
  (upper "./data")                       ; writes, kept
  (command "/bin/sh"))
```

The upper is a key of its own, beside `(mutable overlay)`, where the design first had `(mutable (overlay (upper …)))`: that would have made `mutable` a record in every engine's reading of it, for no more than one key says.

- **chroot, built:** overlayfs in the machine's own namespaces, its lower layers the source and then each of `layers`, the last topmost, its upper `./data`, made if missing, which a user namespace may mount on 5.11 and later. A removal of what a lower layer has is a whiteout in `./data`, a 0:0 device node, and its directories carry `user.overlay.*` xattrs, both the user's to read and remove. overlayfs wants the upper and its work directory on one mount, so the directory holding both is cloned once; the work directory, `.data.hull-work` beside it, is hull's, and is made the user's own group and not setgid, since overlayfs refuses a work directory of a group the namespace does not map, `EPERM`, which is what one inherited from a shared setgid directory is; the upper itself it takes in any group. Each layer, and the upper, is cloned while its path is still the host's. **Measured** in `test/cli_test.sh`: a template beneath a layer, the layer's file winning, an upper with a file of its own, a write landing in the upper and a removal there as a whiteout, the template and the layer untouched, and a second run seeing what the first left; an upper inside a directory setgid to another of the user's groups working; layers without an overlay refused. **Sabotage:** baseline first, in scratch copies: layers ignored, their order reversed so the template won, the upper left in memory, the work directory left in its inherited group, and layers allowed without an overlay, each failed a named case.
- **vm and emu, built the same day:** the guest's overlayfs cannot take a 9p share as its upper, so the overlay is mounted host-side: qemu's child unshares a mount namespace beside the user namespace it already runs in, stages the source, each layer and the upper's directory in the machine's runtime directory by `open_tree` clones, mounts the overlay at `merged` there, a tmpfs being the upper where none is kept, and qemu shares `merged`, writable, which the agent then mounts as a directory in place rather than laying its own overlay. The work directory is made as on chroot. The staging directories are empty on the host, their mounts having been in qemu's namespace, and the engine removes them by name with the machine's directory. **Measured** in `test/qemu_test.sh`: the chroot case on a vm, the layer winning, the write and the whiteout in the upper and the template untouched, and a second vm seeing the first's; the first run left two machine directories, the staging not yet removed, which the suite's last case caught. **Sabotage:** qemu sharing the source rather than `merged`, the agent laying its overlay anyway, and the staging directories left unremoved were each caught, the second by the template being written and the third by the leftover-directory check.
- **docker:** refused, its writable layer being the daemon's.

**Layered volumes, asked for by the holder 2026-10-23 and built the same day:** the stack above, at any path in the machine, as many as are needed, each with its own layers and its own upper or none, so that a machine is assembled from plain directories and nothing is ever rolled into an image file to give it a writable or layered part. The root's `(layers …)` and `(upper …)` are the same thing for `/`.

```scheme
(machine
  (source "dir:./rootfs")
  (mutable overlay)
  (volumes
    (volume (guest "/etc")                 ; a template, a site layer, edits kept
            (layers "dir:./etc-template" "dir:./etc-site")
            (upper "./etc-data"))
    (volume (guest "/var/lib/app")         ; a seed, its writes in memory
            (layers "dir:./seed"))
    (volume (guest "/usr/share/docs")      ; two read-only layers, merged
            (layers "dir:./docs" "dir:./docs-local")
            (mode ro))
    (volume (host "./srv") (guest "/srv"))))
```

A volume is a `host` directory or a stack of `layers`, never both; `upper` needs `layers`, and a volume of `(mode ro)` keeps no writes, so it takes none; each is refused by the spec, naming the key. A read-only stack of two or more layers is an overlay with no upper; one of a single layer is that layer bound read-only, since overlayfs refuses a single lower layer with no upper. A guest path that is not in the machine is made where the root is an overlay, whose upper takes it, and refused in a directory used in place or under a read-only root, where it would be made in the user's own tree, naming it as the guest path, which raidcfgd's message of 2026-10-22 asked for, as Docker makes a mount point. `engine/stack.c` stages one stack for both engines, by `open_tree` clones of each layer and of the upper's directory, in a directory of its own: `lower0`, `lower1`, … and `kept` or `mem`, its tmpfs, and `merged`; its work directory is made as the root's. overlayfs leaves a `work` directory in the work directory, mode 000, which the user could not then remove: the engine gives it back, 0700, when the machine is destroyed, overlayfs making it again on each mount. Measured beforehand in a probe: an overlay mounted by `fsopen` in a user namespace refuses, `EINVAL`, an upper on a detached mount and lower layers that are detached clones, so the stack is staged on attached mounts rather than built from descriptors.
- **chroot:** each stack is staged in the machine's runtime directory, made for it, under a tmpfs mounted there in the machine's own namespace, so that on the host it stays empty; staged before the root's overlay, while the host's paths are still to be seen, and moved to its guest path after `pivot_root`, as a plain volume is. Many uids are not mapped where a volume's upper is kept, as where the root's is, since an owner other than the user would then be one on the user's disk. **Measured** in `test/cli_test.sh`, on a root used in place: a stack at `/mnt` of the template and the site above, the site winning, the upper's own file read, a write landing in the upper alone and nowhere else, the removal seen by a later run; with no upper, a write made and found on no disk; read-only of two layers and of one, read and refused a write; the kept upper's work directory left enterable; no machine directory left; and a volume's missing guest path refused in place and made above an overlay, the dir keeping no trace.
- **vm and emu:** each stack is staged in `v0`, `v1`, … of the machine's directory, in the mount namespace qemu's child unshares for the root's overlay, and qemu shares its `merged` as the volume, read-only where its mode is; the agent mounts it as any volume, making a missing guest path where the root can be written. The staging is removed by name with the machine's directory. **Measured** in `test/qemu_test.sh`: the same stack at `/stk`, kept, in memory and read-only, as on chroot, and the kept upper's work directory left enterable.
- **docker:** refused, `volumes.layers`, the overlay being one the daemon would have to mount.
- **Sabotage:** baseline first, in scratch copies, its qemu suite passing whole: on chroot, the volume bound from its first layer rather than its stack, the guest path never made, the work directory left at 000, `host` allowed beside `layers`, `volumes.layers` never asked, and the layers in reverse order; on a vm, qemu sharing the first layer rather than `merged`, and the staging left unremoved. Each failed a named case.
- **One uid, with an upper on disk:** an owner other than the user written there would be a subordinate id's file on the user's disk, which §7's decision rules out, so such a machine is mapped as one uid.

Images and directories are interconvertible: `hull commit` on a dir-source machine produces a manifest, and `hull materialize <ref> ./dir` unpacks any image — including any pulled Docker Hub image — into a directory you can then run directly, edit, and re-commit. Directories are the working currency; manifests are optional packaging for shipping, signing, snapshots, and dedup.

### 3.8 Deployment & CI without images

The dominant pipeline today is: build → `docker build` (re-tar the artifacts you just built) → push to a registry → pull on the target → unpack → run. Hull's equivalent deletes the middle entirely:

```sh
# CI job
make build DESTDIR=out/rootfs                       # or unpack any release tarball
rsync -a --delete out/rootfs/ deploy@host:/srv/app/rootfs/
ssh deploy@host hull restart app                    # hull.conf: (source "dir:/srv/app/rootfs")
```

- **The deployable unit is a directory.** Transport is anything that moves files; rsync's delta transfer is strictly better granularity than image layers, and `hull check` in CI guarantees the tree runs on every backend you care about — without a registry, auth tokens, image GC policy, or tag-mutation surprises anywhere in the pipeline.
- **Rollback is a directory too:** keep N previous trees and point the spec (or a symlink) back at one. Instant, and you can diff the two trees with standard tools to see exactly what a rollback changes.
- **Versioning lives where it already lives** — your VCS and CI artifact storage — instead of in a second content-addressed system with its own lifecycle.
- **Staging equals production mechanics:** the same rsync'd tree runs under `ns` on a laptop, `vm` on the server, `emu` in a cross-arch CI matrix. The files never change shape.

When a team later wants dedup across many machines, signed snapshots, or to publish to an OCI registry, the optional store (§§3.1–3.6) bolts on without changing this workflow — `hull commit` bridges a directory into it at any time.

### 3.9 Projections — when a real image is genuinely needed

Hull's model admits an honest tension: composefs is a binary metadata blob over files stored elsewhere, and virtiofs is a filesystem-shaped protocol over host files — both *simulate* a filesystem while the truth lives in a directory. Some consumers need the real thing. The reconciliation is to make how a tree is **presented** a first-class, explicit concept, governed by one principle:

> **The files are the single source of truth. Everything image-shaped is a derived projection: content-addressed, cached, regenerable, never authored directly, and never allowed to diverge silently.**

One tree, three projections:

- **`bind`** (ns) — the files themselves, via idmapped bind mount. Nothing simulated.
- **`passthrough`** (vm/emu) — virtiofs; a protocol in the middle, but still the same live files. Metadata blobs used here (the composefs image in `mounts/`) are disposable caches, regenerable from the manifest or tree at any time — they never hold state of their own, and `hull verify` regenerates and compares if one is ever in doubt.
- **`block`** — a real filesystem image, actually built (deterministic mkfs from the tree, systemd-repart-style), for consumers that legitimately need one: the virtualbox/vmware/hyperv engines (no virtiofs), `full`-profile boots wanting a partition table and bootloader, and workloads needing block-level fidelity — swap, O_DIRECT, loop mounts inside the guest, filesystem-specific ioctls, mandatory locking.

Rules that keep block projections honest:

1. **Derived, never authoritative.** A block image is keyed by the content hash of its source tree/manifest; identical tree ⇒ identical image bytes (reproducible mkfs), so caching is sound and `hull verify` can prove a cached image still matches. Deleting every projection loses nothing.
2. **The write path must be explicit.** Under a block projection, guest writes land in a qcow2 overlay — not in your directory. Therefore `(mutable direct)` + block projection is **rejected** by the matrix (your files would silently go stale) unless you opt into `(writeback sync-on-stop)`, which syncs the overlay back to the directory on clean shutdown and documents its crash window. There is no configuration in which the directory and the image can quietly disagree about which one is real.
3. **Need is declared, not discovered.** `(projection auto)` (default) lets the plan step choose per engine; a spec that needs passthrough-hostile semantics declares them — `(fidelity inotify flock odirect)` — and the plan either selects a projection satisfying every declared need or rejects with the reason. Fidelity gaps become matrix rows, found at plan time instead of by 2 a.m. breakage.
4. **Self-containment is an export, not a mode.** When a machine must leave Hull — boot under plain QEMU, ship to a cloud, run under VirtualBox on someone else's laptop — `hull export --bootable <machine> disk.img` emits a standalone image (partition table, bootloader, kernel, tree) as a deliberate border artifact, exactly like OCI push: a copy that leaves the truth behind, and says so.

### 3.10 Media sources — file collections for what we used to store as images

The motivating case: DOS-era games installed from CD-ROM, frequently with copy protection that reads deliberately damaged sectors, subchannel data, or a nonstandard TOC. Until now the only faithful container was a *more* powerful image format — BIN/CUE, raw dumps — an opaque blob precisely because a filesystem can't express those quirks. Hull generalizes §3.9 from filesystems to **media**: store the disc as a collection of plain files, where the structure a filesystem can't hold — and the quirks and unstructured binary data — are *also* files.

```
game/
  hull.conf                 # (backend dosbox)
  c/                        # the installed game: plain files, the DOS C: drive
  cd/
    files/                  # the disc's ISO9660 tree, plain files
    media.conf              # TOC: track layout, modes, pregaps, volume id, timestamps
    tracks/02-audio.wav     # audio tracks as ordinary audio files
    quirks/
      subchannel.bin        # subchannel stream (the copy-protection carrier)
      bad-sectors.conf      # addresses expected to error, and how
      sectors/0x1A40.raw    # raw overrides where mastered bytes ≠ file bytes
```

```scheme
(machine
  (source "dir:./c")            ; the saves (below)
  (media
    (medium
      (type cdrom)
      (source "dir:./cd"))))    ; or "tar:./cd.tar"
```

- **Projection does the reconciling.** The engine needs a disc, so Hull compiles the collection into a bit-faithful virtual disc on demand — derived, content-keyed, disposable; §3.9's rules apply unchanged. Copy-protection checks pass because bad sectors, subchannel data, and the TOC are replayed from the quirk files at the right addresses. And read-only media has no write-path problem at all — the game's *saves* go to `c/`, which is an ordinary dir source with `direct` writes.
- **Quirks are formally a patch.** The definition is exact: `naive = project(files, media.conf)` is deterministic; `quirks = delta(original, naive)`, computed at import and stored as addressed byte/sector patches; reconstruction is `naive + quirks`, verified against the recorded hash of the original dump. Three consequences. *Import is automatic:* `hull media import disc.bin` extracts the files, projects naively, diffs at sector level — whatever fails to round-trip **is** the quirk set; nobody authors quirk files by hand. *Losslessness is provable:* `hull media verify` reconstructs and compares hashes, so converting an image to a file collection is demonstrably exact, not hopefully exact. *Projector versions are frozen:* a patch is only stable against a stable base, so projection algorithms are versioned and immutable once shipped — `media.conf` names the projector version its quirks patch against, new versions ship alongside old ones (like compression formats), and `hull media rebase` re-derives the quirk set against a newer projector as an explicit, hash-verified operation.
- **Graceful degradation ladder.** Best case, everything is meaningful files. Middle case, files plus small binary quirk sidecars. Worst case — a protection scheme that defeats reconstruction — one honest `raw.bin` blob *as a file inside the collection*. The worst case equals today's status quo; the typical case is a diffable, greppable, dedup-friendly directory where the game's files are just files.
- **`tar:` sources.** Any dir-shaped source can be a tarball (`(source "tar:./game.tar")`), transparently unpacked to a content-keyed cache — a transport convenience, not another format.
- **The pattern generalizes.** Floppies (files + boot sector + interleave quirks), cartridge dumps (blob + header metadata as files), and media nobody has thought of yet: sidecar schemas are per media type and versioned, and each engine declares which media types it accepts in its capability table — a dosbox engine accepts `cdrom` and `floppy`; a vm engine accepts `cdrom` (as an emulated drive) and `disk`. §3.11 states the generalization in full.

### 3.11 The universal decomposition — structure + patch

§3.9 and §3.10 are two instances of one law, and the law is Hull's actual foundation:

> **Every opaque binary artifact is stored as `project(structured files) + patch`, where the projector is deterministic and version-frozen, the patch is the recorded delta to the original, and `hash(project(structure) + patch) == hash(original)` is checkable at any time.**

| Artifact | Structure (plain files) | Projector | Patch typically covers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Container image (OCI) | file tree + manifest | layer serializer | layer boundaries + compression quirks (only when digest-exact round-trip is wanted, §3.5) |
| Filesystem image | file tree + fs metadata (type, UUID, label, flags) | deterministic mkfs | bootloader gaps, hidden/unallocated residue, layout the rebuild doesn't reproduce |
| Partitioned disk (appliance firmware, SD-card image) | per-partition trees + partition-table file | repart-style builder | MBR gap bytes, vendor blobs between partitions |
| CD-ROM | ISO tree + TOC + audio track files | ISO/cue builder | subchannel data, bad sectors, raw sector overrides |
| Floppy / cartridge | files (or blob) + metadata | per-format builder | interleave, headers, undecoded regions |

What the generalization buys:

- **One import algorithm for everything.** `hull import <artifact>` is always the same shape: identify format → extract structure → project deterministically → diff against the original → store structure + patch. The reverse — reassembly — *is* the projector. New formats are new projector plugins, not new architectures.
- **The patch size is a measurement of understanding.** A perfect projector yields an empty patch; an unknown format yields patch = whole artifact (the honest blob, no worse than the status quo). Everything between is quantified opacity. Projector improvements shrink patches monotonically via `rebase`, and can never lose data — every rebase is hash-verified against the reconstructed original.
- **Dedup and transport come free.** Structure dedupes in the CAS like all other files; patches are usually tiny; rsync moves both efficiently. A thousand appliance images that share a base OS share it file-by-file, which no image-granularity store can do.
- **Everything is files all the way down.** Greppable, diffable, `git`-able, auditable with standard tools — the project's first goal, now holding even for artifacts that were designed to be opaque.

Invariants, restated once: projectors are deterministic; projector versions are immutable once shipped and named by the collections that depend on them; every decomposition is round-trip verified at import and re-verifiable forever; and patches are themselves ordinary addressed files — opacity is contained, never structural.

### 3.12 Projectors as descriptions — the situ layer

Projectors are not primarily code. A related project, **situ**, describes arbitrary protocols, file formats, and memory structures exhaustively in a description file, and generates accessor code (and more) from the description. Hull adopts it as the substrate of §3.11:

- **A projector = a situ description + an optional policy module.** The situ spec supplies the parser (import/decompose), the serializer (reassemble), and the very definition of "structure" for a format. Purely declarative formats — partition tables, cue/TOC layouts, headers, cartridge formats — need *no* procedural code at all: a new projector is a new situ file. Only formats with genuine policy (ext4's block allocation in mkfs, ISO9660 sector layout choices) add a small deterministic policy module alongside.
- **The closure:** the patch is, by construction, *exactly what the situ description does not yet describe*. Patch size across a corpus becomes a live completeness metric for the description; refining a situ spec measurably shrinks patches (via `rebase`, hash-verified, risk-free). "Quantified opacity" stops being a metaphor and becomes a number attached to a description file.
- **Projector versioning inherits from situ:** the frozen-version invariant maps to pinned situ description versions; a collection's `media.conf` names the description (and its hash) it was decomposed against.
- **Everything else that speaks bytes uses the same layer.** The agent control protocol (Appendix B), the manifest canonical encoding (Appendix A), and sidecar schemas can all be situ descriptions, with accessors generated for every component — C core, Qt GUI, TUI, guest agent — from one source of truth. One description language ends the codec-drift problem between components, and situ's ancillary generators (validators, pretty-printers, fuzzers) give `hull inspect <artifact>` and protocol fuzzing essentially for free.

**Decided 2026-10-09: Appendices A and B are situ descriptions.** The manifest is `manifest/manifest.situ` (A.2), the agent's messages are `agent/protocol.situ` (B.3) and their framing `agent/message.situ` (B.2), each held to its committed capability map by `make contract` and, for the first two, to bytes it must accept and refuse by `make test`. The appendices summarise the schemas and carry what a layout cannot state. Generator targets wait for the code that consumes them: so far only the framing generates C.

### 3.13 Representations — one identity, three forms

A machine's content has exactly one identity (its manifest hash / tree hash) and three interchangeable representations. Conversions between them are lossless, deterministic, and hash-verified — §3.11 applied to Hull's own formats.

**1. The in-fs format (the collection).** The formalized on-filesystem layout — a versioned, situ-described spec, not a convention: the payload tree as plain files, plus a `.hull/` metadata directory carrying what the filesystem itself can't: ownership records (mirroring the `user.hull.owner` xattrs, for filesystems or transports that strip xattrs), media sidecars and quirks (§3.10), manifest fragments, and the format version. Rule: **the payload tree must remain useful with `.hull/` deleted** — metadata enriches, never captures. This is the working representation: rsync it, `git` it, edit it live.

**2. The Hull image file (`.hull` pack) — the efficient single-file form.** For when a directory is inconvenient (release artifacts, download links, cold storage, email-a-machine). Not a tarball with a new suffix — a format designed for its job:

- **Canonical:** deterministic serialization — the same collection always produces byte-identical packs, so the pack's hash *is* the content identity (tar can't promise this; ordering, padding, and metadata make it non-canonical).
- **Structured:** header + manifest index + content-defined-chunked, zstd-compressed object pack + patch section. situ-described end to end, so accessors, validators, and `hull inspect` come from the description.
- **Seekable & mountable:** the index is upfront; single files are extractable without reading the pack, and the pack is directly mountable (composefs/FUSE) — a pack can *be* a machine source (`(source "pack:./app.hull")`) without unpacking.
- **Dedup-aware transport:** chunk boundaries are content-defined (§C), so transferring a pack to a host that has a previous version moves only changed chunks; two packs sharing content share chunks in the store.
- `hull pack <dir> <file.hull>` / `hull unpack` round-trip exactly; `tar:` remains accepted as a lowest-common-denominator input, never an output format.

**3. Foreign image files — load and import other software's formats.** Two modes, both engine-visible in the matrix:

- **Import** — the §3.11 one-algorithm path: qcow2, VMDK, VHD/VHDX, VDI, raw disks, ISO/BIN-CUE, OVA/OVF appliances, OCI/docker-save tarballs, Vagrant boxes → decompose into a collection (files + metadata + patch), provably lossless, re-exportable to the original format bit-exact when the projector permits.
- **Attach** — run a foreign image *in place*, no conversion: `(source "image:./disk.vmdk")` hands the file to an engine that speaks it natively (vmware↔VMDK, hyperv↔VHDX, qemu↔qcow2/raw, virtualbox↔VDI). Attach trades away inspectability and dir-workflows for zero-cost onboarding of existing VMs — declared per engine in its capability table, and `hull import` is always one command away when the user wants the files-first benefits.

### 3.14 Why files beat loop-mounted images — the technical case

Hull supports image workflows fully (§3.9, §3.13). But the architecture's strength is the **strict separation of files from loop-mounted images**, and the case rests on mechanism, not taste:

**Safer.** Loop-mounting an image makes the host kernel's filesystem driver parse untrusted metadata — ext4/xfs/btrfs parsers are a notorious privilege-escalation surface, which is why mounting requires root, why rootless runtimes refuse it, and why auto-mounting images has been a recurring CVE class. A directory of files requires no filesystem parser at all: reading files is just reading files. Where Hull does need a mounted image format, it uses exactly one — read-only EROFS/composefs with fs-verity — a single, small, audited, unwritable parser instead of the full read-write zoo. And files are transparent to scanning, auditing, and integrity tooling; a block image is a blob those tools must be taught to crack open.

**Faster.** The host page cache dedupes at file granularity: twenty machines sharing a base tree over virtiofs/CAS hold **one** copy of each shared file in host RAM, while twenty loop-mounted images cache twenty copies of the same bytes — the kernel cannot know identical blocks in distinct block devices are identical. Files avoid the fs-in-file-on-fs stack: no nested journaling (journal-on-journal write amplification), no image-internal fragmentation, working host readahead, reflink/hardlink dedup for free. And directories have no size: no pre-allocated disk, no full-disk-inside-half-full-image waste, no resize2fs ceremony, no fsck of a 40 GB blob after a crash.

**More useful.** Files are *shareable between machines while live* — the same read-only tree mounted into many VMs and containers simultaneously, which a block image fundamentally cannot do safely (multi-attach of writable images corrupts; even read-only multi-attach is fraught). Files are incrementally backed up by any tool ever written — rsync, borg, git, tape — at file granularity, where an image is an all-or-nothing blob whose smallest change invalidates the whole artifact for dumb tools and demands specialized block-diffing for smart ones. Files are indexable, greppable, deduplicable, patchable in place, diffable across versions and across *machines*. Every one of these properties exists because the unit of storage matches the unit of meaning.

The design consequence, stated once: **block projections are a border facility for consumers that genuinely need block semantics (§3.9) — never the resting state.** Data at rest in Hull is files; images are made on demand, used, and discarded.

### 3.15 Flash for embedded update testing — a design option, not planned

**Recorded 2026-10-22 at the holder's asking, "write it down as this may need doing later"; no project needs it now, and whether and when it is built is the holder's.** The question was how to test full-system updates of embedded devices realistically against NOR, NAND or eMMC, which qemu models only thinly. What a realistic test exercises is the update tool's own path, RAUC, SWUpdate or Mender over MTD, UBI and UBIFS or over an eMMC's boot partitions, and above all a power cut partway through an update, survived across the reboot. Three levels, cheapest first:

1. **In a vm, with what the host's kernel already has.** Measured on this host, 6.12: `nandsim`, `mtdram`, `block2mtd`, `ubi`, `ubifs` and `mtdblock` are modules, so a hull vm loads them since the guest has the host's modules (§4.11). `nandsim` takes a chip's ID bytes for its geometry and injects bad blocks, bit flips, and pages or blocks that fail after so many writes, erases or reads; UBI's debugfs adds emulated power cuts, bit flips and I/O failures; `block2mtd` makes a NOR-like MTD of a block device; an eMMC's user area is a virtio disk with partitions. **The weak point is the power cut**: the flash's state has to outlive the guest, as `nandsim`'s cache file on a disk image, and whether killing the vm loses exactly what a cut to real flash loses, no more and no less, is unmeasured. That measurement comes first, and decides level 2.
2. **A hull medium for flash**, a `(medium (type nand|nor|emmc) …)` (J.4) with its geometry, its image a file on the host, and faults to inject by spec, power cut at the Nth program or erase among them, so that a test is a spec rather than a script.
3. **Through the bootloader**, for U-Boot's environment, boot counting and falling back after a failed boot: a board model with firmware, as qemu's ARM `virt` gives, with real CFI NOR in `pflash` kept in a host file and SD or eMMC through an SDHCI controller. This host's `qemu-system-x86_64` 10.0 offers `sdhci-pci` and `sd-card` and no eMMC device; `qemu-system-aarch64` is not installed here.

**eMMC's hardware partitions are the part nothing models.** An eMMC splits its one flash array through its EXT_CSD register: two boot partitions, an RPMB area for replay-protected authenticated writes, up to four general-purpose partitions, and an enhanced (SLC-mode) area of the user partition. Partitioning is set once, finalised by `PARTITION_SETTING_COMPLETED`, and takes effect at the next power cycle. Linux shows the result as `mmcblk0boot0`, `boot1`, `mmcblk0rpmb` and `mmcblk0gp0` to `gp3`; `mmc-utils` and the update tools drive it through the `MMC_IOC_CMD` ioctl, RAUC's `boot-emmc` slots flipping `BOOT_PARTITION_ENABLE` for A and B. Linux has no simulator for an eMMC as it has `nandsim` for NAND. Upstream qemu has an eMMC card model in its SD code, made for ARM BMC boards, with boot partitions, and RPMB work followed; which release carries which was not checked, and the general-purpose partitions, the enhanced area and the one-time partitioning flow are not believed modelled. **The way to it is extending qemu's model:** EXT_CSD's settings mapping regions of one backing image to boot, general-purpose, user and RPMB, switched by `PARTITION_ACCESS`; the once-only settings kept in a file beside the image so that a vm's restart is the power cycle that applies them and they cannot be made twice; RPMB's key, write counter and HMAC; and write-protect groups and the boot partitions' power-on write protection where an update relies on them. qemu's SD bus has no out-of-process interface, so this is a patch to qemu, carried against a vendored qemu and offered upstream whole, as beerssh's `vterm-patch/` is for libvterm. **The cheap stand-in**, separate virtio disks named for boot0, boot1 and the general-purpose partitions, tests an update that only writes named devices, and cannot exercise the EXT_CSD ioctls or RPMB that the eMMC paths of the update tools use.

---

## 4. The spec: `hull.conf`

One file describes a machine. Multi-machine files, compose-style, are the same schema under `(machines (<name> …) …)`.

```scheme
(machine
  (source "registry.example.com/webapp:2.1")  ; an image ref…
  ; (source "dir:./rootfs")                   ; …or just a directory of files (§3.7)
  ; (mutable direct)                          ; direct | overlay (dir sources default to direct)
  (arch host)                    ; host | amd64 | arm64 | riscv64 … (foreign arch ⇒ emu, or ns+binfmt)
  (command "./server" "--port" "80")
  (workdir "/app")
  (env (RUST_LOG "info"))
  (env_file ".env")              ; optional, gitignored secrets

  (ports (port (host 8080) (guest 80) (proto tcp)))
  (volumes
    (volume (host "./data") (guest "/var/data") (mode rw))
    (volume (host "./cfg")  (guest "/etc/app")  (mode ro)))

  (cpus 2)
  (memory "2G")

  (backend ns)                   ; a CLASS (ns | vm | emu) or a CONCRETE ENGINE
                                 ; (docker, qemu, virtualbox, vmware, …) — the only line that changes;
