A whiteout would meet the same wall: it is a 0:0 character device, which the user cannot make. `security_model=mapped-xattr` keeps modes and devices in xattrs and might pass both, at the price of an upper layer whose files on the host are not what they are in the machine. Not tried. Writes kept on the host, past the machine's end, are what `hull commit` will want (§3.7).

**A machine's network out is passt's, as the user; its ports in are the engine's.** §8 names user-mode networking per VM. The engine starts a passt for each machine on a socket pair, and the other end is qemu's virtio-net device.
- **The addresses are fixed,** in both the engine and the agent: the guest is 10.0.2.15/24, its gateway 10.0.2.2, and its DNS 10.0.2.3, which passt forwards to the host's own resolver. These are slirp's well-known addresses. The agent brings up `lo` and `eth0` and adds the route itself, by ioctl, since there is no DHCP client in the guest.
- **resolv.conf:** the machine's `/etc/resolv.conf` has the agent's file, which names 10.0.2.3, bound over it, as docker writes its own. It is read-only when the root is. The bind is only over a regular file: a link would be followed in the agent's root, outside the machine.
- **The host's loopback is out of reach.** By default passt makes the gateway address the host's 127.0.0.1, so a guest could reach whatever listens there for the user alone. `--no-map-gw` turns that off. A vm-class machine is the boundary §8 and Appendix D put the risky machine behind, and an open path to the host's loopback would undo it.
- **Why not qemu's own `-netdev user`:** slirp has the same mapping and no way to turn it off.
- **Why passt does not forward the ports.** Tried by hand with `--no-map-gw`, a connection to a port passt forwarded from 127.0.0.1 reached passt and never the guest. passt has no guest-side address to give a connection from the host's loopback. The address that would, `--map-host-loopback`, works both ways: passt(1) says packets from the guest to it go to the host's loopback, and it has no inbound-only form.
- **No pid file, fixed 2026-10-21.** The engine passed `--pid` with a file in the machine's directory, under `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`. Debian's AppArmor profile for passt lets it write only under `/tmp` and the home directory, so with the runtime directory where it always is, `/run/user/UID`, passt refused the pid file, and no qemu machine started: `Couldn't open PID file …: Permission denied`. The suites never saw it, because their runtime directories are under `$BUILD_DIR`, in the home directory. The pid was only for `hull stats`, so the engine now finds the daemon as the process whose fd 3 is the far end of its socket pair, by inode in `/proc`. Found while giving qemu secrets, whose case needs a tmpfs runtime directory and so failed the same way under `/dev/shm`; that case now covers it.
- **A kept machine outlives its core, and the next core takes it back** (§4.10's adoption), written 2026-10-21.
  - **qemu** is spawned without the parent-death signal under `(on_disconnect keep)`, so it outlives a core killed or crashed; a machine not kept still dies with it. `persist()` gives qemu's pid, the vsock context id, the machine's directory, the agent's token, passt's pid, the address and the ports, as text, a line each.
  - **The agent** under keep leaves the workload running when its host goes, ends the execs and forwarded connections whose frontends went with it, and listens again. What the workload writes meanwhile is held, its last 256 KiB, for the host that resumes; a workload that ends meanwhile is reported to that host, and the machine powers off once it has been. So a kept VM whose workload has ended waits, holding its memory, until a core comes, as a stopped docker container waits.
  - **A token, since any local user can reach a guest's vsock port.** The engine gives the agent 32 random bytes with `start`; a host after the first is answered `hello` and nothing else until it says `resume` with them, compared in constant time, and is dropped otherwise, the agent listening on. A kept VM can wait for its next core for as long as the user stays away, which is a much longer window than the first connection's at boot. That window, before the engine's first connect, is as it was: B.2's claim that vsock needs no authentication holds for it only as far as the race does.
  - **Taking it back:** the pid must still be a qemu whose command line names the machine's directory, checked through a pidfd opened first, which then stands for it, as it is init's child now. The engine dials the agent, says `resume` with the token, hears `started` and the held output, binds the ports again on the address, and runs its reader and forwarder as at a start. The old core's log is gone with it, so `hull logs` has only what was held. The sweep of dead cores' directories passes over the directories of machines taken back, whose names would otherwise fall to it.
  - **Failure modes:** a pid that is not that qemu is gone, and forgotten; an agent that does not answer or will not resume is left, for a later core, not stopped, as it is not this core's.
  - **An exit is told only to a believed host, found by its case.** The agent's loop settles an ended workload on every turn, so the first version told `exited` to whichever host connected next, before its hello was answered and whether or not it had the token, then powered off. The core taking the machine back heard `exited` where it expected `hello`, and the exit was lost; an unbelieved process would have read it. It now waits for `resume`.
  - **Measured** in `test/qemu_test.sh`: the file written, qemu alive after its core's SIGKILL, a host without the token dropped although it asked for an exec and told nothing but hello, the machine taken back by the core `hull ps` starts, the port forwarded again, an exec, another machine booting meanwhile without sweeping its directory, what was written while no core held it, the exit through `attach`, and the file and qemu gone after; a workload that ended while no core held it, its exit 7 heard by the next core and the VM then gone; and a kept file whose pid is another process's, forgotten, that process unharmed. A machine not kept still dies with its core.
  - **Sabotage,** baseline first. Breaking each of these failed a named case: the parent-death signal kept under keep, any host believed, the output not held, the ports not bound again, the exit not kept for the next host, the exit told to a host not believed, the directory swept, and the pid not checked. The held output and the kept exit first survived: the workload wrote `during` only after the new core had it, and no case had a workload end with no core. The test now waits four seconds after the kill, and the second case was added.
- **Every suite run once with its runtime directory at `/run/user/UID`, 2026-10-21,** in a scratch copy, to look for more of the pid file's kind: a fault that hides because the suites' runtime directories are under `$BUILD_DIR`. `make test`, `check-qemu`, `check-docker` and `check-flip` all passed, but for the cli case that wants the secrets refused in a runtime directory not in memory, which `/run/user` is. That case now says so and is not counted when the suite's runtime directory is a tmpfs, as it would be with `$BUILD_DIR` under a tmpfs `/tmp`, rather than failing there.
- **Its life:** passt's parent exits once passt is set up, so the engine knows whether it started and, if not, why, from `passt.log`. The daemon it leaves ends when qemu's end of the pair closes (`--one-off`), so it goes with qemu, and qemu goes with the core.
- **The cost:** a LAN host at 10.0.2.15 is out of the machine's reach, since that is the machine's own address. This host's LAN is 10.0.0.0/16, which holds it. A host without passt can run no qemu machine. That is a hard requirement, as `/dev/vhost-vsock` is, rather than a machine silently started with no network.
- **Measured:** boot time is unchanged, about 2.2 s.

**The guest agent is `agent/agent.c`,** built static as `hull-agent`, beside `hull`, where the engine looks for it. It is PID 1 and follows B.1:
- It mounts `/dev`, `/proc` and `/sys`, loads the modules `/hull/modules` lists, and listens on vsock port 1 for the one host connection.
- On `spec` it applies the machine: the hostname, the directory's share mounted at `/machine` and read-only if asked, each volume's share at its guest path, and `/proc`, `/dev` and `/sys` inside wherever the directory has a place for them. Then it moves `/machine` onto `/` and chroots, as switch_root does.
- On `start` it forks the workload, in a session of its own, with the spec's environment, workdir and `no_new_privs`. A command that cannot be run exits 127, as on chroot.
- The workload's stdout and stderr come back as `data` on channel 0, which B.3 already uses for the workload in `signal`.
- **Its end:** once the workload is reaped and its pipes read, the agent says `exited`, exactly as the workload ended, kills whatever is left, says `bye`, and powers off once the host has closed the connection, which the host does on reading `bye`, or after 3 seconds (§4.10). A pipe an orphan still holds is read for what it has, and not waited on.
- **`stop`** is SIGTERM, then SIGKILL at its timeout. A host that goes away is `(on_disconnect stop)` with nobody to tell. `signal` forwards the seven names the frontend protocol does, spelled with or without `SIG`.

**The host's side,** in the engine:
- `start` picks a random vsock context id and boots qemu. A context id is the host's, shared by every VM, so if qemu refuses it as taken another is tried, four times in all.
- It connects to the agent while qemu lives, for up to 60 s, then runs hello, `spec` with the plan's own `(machine …)`, `ready`, `start`, `started`.
- A thread then reads what the agent sends until the connection closes, waits for qemu, and reports the exit as the agent gave it. A machine whose agent never said goes as SIGKILL, with the last line of its console, which is kept in a file, logged as why.
- `stop` asks the agent and kills qemu if it has not powered off within the timeout and five seconds more. `hull kill` is the agent's `signal`.
- **qemu has `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG`,** so it dies with the core, as a chroot machine does, and inherits none of the core's descriptors.
- **A core killed outright leaves its machines' directories.** The next core's first machine removes them: one core runs per user, holding `core.lock`, so a directory named for a pid that is not running was a dead core's. Its files are removed by name.

**What it declares** is what that does: a dir source in place or as an overlay, `command`, `workdir`, `env`, `hostname`, `volumes`, `cpus`, `memory`, `readonly_root`, `no_new_privs`, both `on_disconnect` values and `emu.cores_scale`, with the two emulated defaults above. `ports` is declared emulated, as §6 has it: tcp and udp, forwarded from the machine's address (§4.10).
- **What it no longer declares:** image refs, ssh, media, device passthrough, the full profile and the other kernels. The draft declared all of these from §5 and §6 before any of it was written, the way docker's did.
  - **A caller stood at `vm.kernel.path` on 2026-10-24, and it is built since 2026-10-25** (above). By netcfgd's message of 2026-10-24: it wanted a vm booting a kernel built without `CONFIG_NFT_NAT` and `CONFIG_NFT_MASQ`, so that a construct's absence could not be confused with a blocked autoload, to check the errno its rule list reports. The engine built only `bundled`, `/boot/vmlinuz-<release>`, so netcfgd drove qemu itself for that one tier, three kernels differing only in those configs with `CONFIG_MODULES` off, and an initramfs from the kernel tree's own `gen_init_cpio`; it agreed exactly with a hull vm that blocks autoload by an empty module table (its `natcap` rig, which found the errno is `ENOENT`, not `EOPNOTSUPP`). Two things it learned, now provided for: an unprivileged runner needs `/dev/console` in the initramfs before PID 1 (a user cannot `mknod` one; the engine's initramfs carries it), and `CONFIG_IKCONFIG_PROC` lets the payload prove from inside which kernel it booted, as `--version` proves the binary. netcfgd's `tests/kernel/` builds the three (k0 control, k1 no chain type, k2 no masquerade), and it will point that at `(kernel "path:...")` to replace its hand-driven qemu and report what the three states say.
- **The cost:** §4's example, which has an image source, a port and `(ssh true)`, is refused on vm and emu for the image and ssh. A foreign arch is refused on emu.
- **Not written yet:** the guest's own view of its stats (§4.10). A machine's output reaches the core, and so `hull logs`, as a chroot machine's does.

**`hull exec` on a qemu machine is a channel of the agent's,** which the engine allocates per exec.
- **The command** gets the machine's environment, workdir and `no_new_privs`, on pipes or on a pseudo-terminal of the machine's own. The agent mounts `devpts` for that. A terminal is the command's controlling terminal, of the size the request gives.
- **B.3's `exec` carries the size now:** `(tty (cols n) (rows n))` or `(tty false)`. B.3 had `(tty true)` with the size sent after in a `winch`, which the frontend protocol had already found reads 80 by 24 first (§4.10). The schema was changed to the frontend's spelling, and `(tty true)` is a refused vector.
- **Output flows on B.2's credit,** 64 KiB to start. The engine's reader queues what arrives and never waits. Each stream's own thread hands it to `on_data`, which may wait while the frontend is behind, and gives the agent credit back once it has. So a frontend that falls behind holds back its own command and nothing else: the agent does not read a channel out of credit, and the command blocks writing, as on a pipe. A stream queues at most the agent's 64 KiB.
- **stdin flows on the agent's credit the other way.** The agent gives the host 64 KiB, writes from a ring as the command's pipe takes it, and gives credit back as it does. It says `eof` once the command's input has closed. `stream_write` waits for credit, and `stream_eof` ends the wait. An `eof` on a terminal ends nothing, as in chroot.
- **The exit is said once the output has been read,** as for the workload, so nothing the command wrote is lost behind it.
- **`stream_close`** has the agent SIGKILL the command and waits for its exit. Every exec the agent has not reported when the connection ends is reported as SIGKILL, 137: a machine that stops takes its commands, and their frontends hear it at once.
- **Signals and resizes** are the agent's `signal` and `winch` on the exec's channel.

**A machine's ports are forwarded by the engine, over vsock, as connections of the agent's.** passt cannot forward in without opening the host's loopback to the guest (above), and vsock needs no address on either side.
- **The host's side:** the engine binds each port on the machine's address (§4.10) before the machine boots, so a port that is taken refuses the start with its reason: `port 8080 on 127.107.178.190: Address already in use`. A thread per machine accepts. Each connection gets a thread and a channel of the agent's, opened with `(connect (ch n) (port n))`, which B.3 gains for it. The listeners close when the machine ends.
- **The agent's side:** it connects to the machine's own address, 10.0.2.15, on the guest port, as docker's proxy connects to a container's address. So a server listening only on the guest's loopback is not reached, as on docker. The answer is `started` or `error`, and a refused connect reaches the client as a close.
- **Bytes flow as an exec's do,** on B.2's credit both ways, with the socket in place of `on_data` and stdin. A client that does not read holds back the machine's writer, and nothing else.
- **Its ends:** the client shutting its writing is the agent's `eof`, and the agent shuts the machine's side for writing, so a client may half-close. The machine's end, read by the agent, is `exited` with 0, and the host then shuts the client's socket. A client that goes, or resets, has the host send `signal` on the channel, and the agent then resets the machine's side. A machine that ends shuts every client's socket, so a client that does not read cannot hold `destroy`.
- **The reset was found by testing.** The agent first ended an abandoned connection with `shutdown(SHUT_RDWR)`, and a server in the machine writing to it blocked for good. Its socket sat in CLOSE_WAIT with 2.6 MB queued, and the agent's end in FIN_WAIT2. After SHUT_RD, Linux goes on taking what arrives, and the close is then a FIN, so the writer is never told. A linger of zero then a close is a reset, and the writer gets EPIPE.
- **A udp port is a flow per client,** added 2026-10-20. The engine binds a udp socket on the machine's address, and the first datagram from a client's address and port opens a channel with `(connect (ch n) (port n) (proto udp))`. The agent answers it with a udp socket connected to 10.0.2.15 on the guest port, so the server in the machine sees each client as a sender of its own. Each `data` is one datagram, either way, an empty one included, up to 65,536 bytes, in a frame that holds a MiB.
- **Neither way waits for credit:** a datagram the far end cannot take now is dropped, as udp's are. The reader sends the machine's reply to the client with one `sendto` that does not block, so a flood from the machine queues nothing in the host. The agent's socket hears an earlier datagram's ICMP refusal as ECONNREFUSED, which ends nothing, since the client may send again once the server listens.
- **A flow ends after 90 seconds unused,** as docker's proxy keeps one, swept each second while any is open. It also ends if the agent refuses it, and when the machine ends. The host then sends `signal` on its channel, and the agent closes the socket. Flows share the machine's 64 channels with execs and connections, and a datagram that finds none left is dropped and logged.

**Measured,** in `test/qemu_test.sh`, which `make check-qemu` runs, since `make check` needs no KVM. Its 110 cases:
- **One boot,** and every line it prints checked: output on both streams kept apart, the exit status, env, workdir, hostname, uid 0, `no_new_privs`, a session of its own, two CPUs, 256 MiB, and the KVM clock.
- **How a machine ends:** death by a signal, 143, and a missing command, 127 with the reason.
- **Writes:** a write landing in the directory as the user's file, a read-only root, and volumes read-write and read-only.
- **An overlay:** `/` with the directory's owner, group and mode; a write and a removal inside, neither reaching the directory; a 200 MiB write stopping between 90 and 127 MiB; and a read-only overlay refusing a write.
- **A running machine:** listed by `ps`, a TERM through `hull kill` caught by the workload, SIGTERM to `hull run` stopping the machine with 143, and qemu dying with a SIGKILLed core.
- **exec,** in a machine of its own: the machine's environment and hostname, a status, a missing command 127, stdin ended by `/dev/null`, 588,895 bytes of stdin by length and FNV-1a hash, 300 kB to a command that sleeps six seconds first, and 3 MB to one that sleeps three.
- **exec's output:** 3 MB counted against the same probe on the host, and 20 MB to a reader seven seconds behind arriving whole. Meanwhile the command is held, the machine counting one process more two seconds in, and another exec answers within three seconds.
- **stats:** an exec's 300 ms of CPU, 300 more in a child it reaps, and 4 MiB written through 9p, each showing in qemu's figures; at least 32 MiB resident; and a stopped machine refused.
- **exec's terminal and its ends:** a terminal of 100 by 30, spoken by hand, its controlling terminal, resized to 120 by 40; `hull ssh` giving the machine's `/bin/sh` a terminal and the frontend's TERM; a TERM to `hull exec` caught by the command; a frontend SIGKILLed taking its command with it, the machine counting as many processes after; and an exec in a machine that stops ending 137 at once.
- **The network:** the agent's resolv.conf, read-only under a read-only root. `lo` is up: a dial to a port nothing holds is refused rather than unreachable. The host's resolver answers through 10.0.2.3, and a listener on the host's own address is reached. A listener on the host's 127.0.0.1 is not reached through the gateway, checked from both sides. Two host listeners on ports of their own say who came.
- **Ports:** six, each from a free port to the probe. These cases run in order:
  - a greeting and a reply, then the machine's close reaching the client;
  - a port nothing in the machine holds, closed;
  - 3 MB out to a client that reads a second late, matching the probe's own spew on the host by length and FNV-1a;
  - 3 MB in, half-closed and counted by the machine, matching;
  - a client leaving after 100 kB of 20 MB, which the machine's writer hears as a broken pipe;
  - a client holding its connection unread, through a 4 KiB window, while the machine is stopped: the listeners close within four seconds, before that client reads again, and its connection then ends.
- **Ports, around those:** the listeners closed once the machine has gone, a port held on 127.0.0.1 leaving a machine publishing it free to start, the same port taken on the machine's address refusing it.
- **`hull proxy-ssh`:** the machine's undeclared port 22 reached both ways by its name with `.hull.`, and a refusal, 125 with its reason, dialled before the probe listens.
- **A udp port,** to a probe echoing every datagram to its sender: two clients each answered on their own flow, the probe counting two senders; datagrams of 0, 1, 1400 and 65,000 bytes each echoed whole; and a datagram sent while the probe is not listening dropped, while the client's later ones are answered on the same flow: the probe, listening again, sees the client come from the port it came from before. Sabotage, baseline first: breaking each of these failed it. Breaks in the agent: forwarding a refusal as a flow's end, putting datagrams through the byte ring, a 16 KiB datagram buffer, and dropping the empty datagram. Breaks in the engine: one flow for every client, a flow per datagram, and a 16 KiB buffer. The refusal first passed, since a new flow answers as well: the client is simply another sender to the machine. So the case now runs two echoes and compares the ports.
- **The emu class** under TCG, without the KVM clock, as an overlay whose write stays in the machine.
- **Nothing left:** no qemu of the suite's, no passt, and no machine directory, the dead core's included. passt is also checked gone with a SIGKILLed core, and gone with its machine while the core runs on: a machine boots and ends under the core another keeps running, and one passt is left.

**Sabotage,** baseline first, in a scratch copy running `make check-qemu`. Breaking each of these failed a named case:
- **In the agent:** the hostname, the environment, the workdir, `no_new_privs`, the session, mounting volumes, reporting a signal, 127 for a command not there, stderr kept apart, a stop's SIGTERM, and forwarding a signal.
- **In the engine:** memory, CPUs, TCG for emu, sweeping a dead core's directories, and reporting a signal's exit.
- **Read-only, root and volume,** each broken on both sides together: qemu's `readonly=on` and the agent's read-only mount. Either alone holds.
- **qemu's death signal** first passed. A core that dies closes its vsock connection, and the agent then stops the machine and powers off within the same second, which is `(on_disconnect stop)` doing its job. With both removed, the machine outlived its core and the case failed. Each alone holds.
- **Untested:** retrying a vsock context id that is taken, which a test cannot make happen on purpose.

**Sabotage of the overlay,** baseline first, the same way, after one baseline lost to an intermittent (§4.10's open items). Breaking each of these failed a named case: reading the mutability in the agent, the upper root's owner, its mode, the overlay module, and keeping a read-only root off the overlay. Two needed more:
- **qemu's read-only share alone** passes: the agent mounts the share read-only as well. With both broken and the share bound in place of the overlay, the writes reached the directory and the case failed. Each alone holds.
- **The tmpfs removed** passes and cannot fail here. The upper layer then lands on the initramfs's root, which the kernel also makes a tmpfs of half the memory when no `root=` is given. The mount is kept so the bound does not hang on how the kernel makes its root.

**Sabotage of exec,** baseline first, the same way. Breaking each of these failed a named case:
- the agent holding output to its credit, at both of its checks;
- the host giving output credit back;
- the agent giving stdin credit back;
- `eof` ending the input;
- the exit waiting for the output;
- the terminal's first size, and the resize on either side;
- a signal going to its own channel;
- an exec getting the machine's environment;
- `stream_close` killing the command.

Breaking the SIGKILL reported for streams whose agent has gone hung the stopped-machine case, and the suite ran out its time. Two needed more:
- **The host waiting for the agent's stdin credit** first passed. Its largest input to a command not reading was 300 kB, which arrived whole anyway. Tried by hand, 3 MB to a command sleeping three seconds arrived as 131,072 bytes, the rest refused as more input than the credit. That is now a case, and it fails. The same broken engine also delivered 1,174,207 bytes for `seq 1 200000`'s 588,895, about twice. No cause was found, and no path for intact code to overrun the agent's ring was found either. Unexplained, and seen only with the engine broken.
- **`TIOCSCTTY` removed** passed and cannot fail. On Linux a session leader that opens a terminal without `O_NOCTTY` gets it as its controlling terminal, so the open already does what the ioctl says. It is kept for saying so.

**Sabotage of the network,** baseline first, the same way. Breaking each of these failed a named case: `--no-map-gw` (both sides of the loopback case), the default route, eth0's address, the virtio_net module, qemu's netdev, passt's DNS forward, binding resolv.conf, and making it read-only. Three needed more:
- **`lo` left down** first passed. The case wanted any dial refused, and the dial to the gateway is refused too. With `lo` down the dial to 127.0.0.1 times out, and it does not reach the host's loopback, checked by hand. Each dial now names its address in its line.
- **Leaking qemu's end of passt's socket** first passed. passt then lived as long as the core, and every check for one left ran once the core had gone. A machine now boots and ends while another keeps the same core running, and exactly one passt must be left.
- **`--one-off` removed** passed and cannot fail. passt given a connected socket with `--fd` exits when its peer closes, with the option or without it, tried by hand. It is kept for saying so.
- **Untested:** passt failing to start, which a test cannot make happen on purpose.

**Sabotage of the ports,** baseline first, the same way. Breaking each of these failed a named case:
- in the agent: settling a connection, and its half-close;
- in the engine: shutting the client's socket at the end, the credit a connection gives back, the client's end becoming `eof`, and ending the connection once its client has gone;
- checking the listener's bind, and refusing a udp port.

Three needed more:
- **Closing the listeners with the machine** first passed. Once the ports machine was gone, the suite's core idled out, and its exit closed every socket anyway. A second machine now keeps the core running across those cases, and it fails.
- **Shutting each client's socket when the machine ends still passes, and why is not known.** Its job is to free a `deliver()` blocked writing to a client that does not read, so that `destroy()` can end. The case now holds a client with a 4 KiB window, unread, for four seconds before the stop, and requires the listeners closed within four seconds after. Before the stop, the engine's socket had 1.79 MB unsent and two of the core's threads waited in `wait_woken`, one blocked in `send()` and one in `recv()`.
  - **Under strace, the sabotage did what it should:** the main thread ended in a futex wait inside `destroy()`, with the `send()` and `recv()` still blocked. The intact build, traced the same way, shut the socket and returned to its poll.
  - **Untraced,** the sabotaged core had both threads gone within half a second of the stop, and the listeners closed. Nothing found closes or shuts that socket on that path.
  - So the shutdown is shown needed, under a tracer only. What frees the untraced core is open.
- **The count:** of the eleven sabotages, nine fail a named case. `3ba0438`'s message says ten. It was written before the second machine's case was rerun, and that rerun caught only the listeners.
- **The agent's zero linger removed** passed and cannot fail here. Closing a socket with unread data already resets it on Linux, and a writer that is still writing always leaves some. The linger covers the moment a writer has none queued, which a test cannot choose.

---

### 4.12 Pools, leases and hardware — design, not yet built

**Asked for by the holder 2026-10-22: a limited collection of machines that can be allocated and reserved, real hardware among them, behind an interface like a test runner's.** Nothing below is written yet; it is the design to build from, and its open questions are the holder's.

**Why software machines want it too.** Every host has limits a run does not see today: a KVM guest's memory is a hard allocation, docker and chroot machines share the CPUs and the disk, and a CI host wants N jobs at once and no more. hull starts whatever it is asked to, so the host is what gives way. A pool makes the limit a thing the core enforces, and gives software machines and boards on a bench one interface, so a test written against a pool of qemu machines runs unchanged against a pool of real boards.

**The terms, one word each:**
- **pool** — a named set of slots and a capacity: `ci`, `boards`.
- **slot** — one place a machine can run: for a software pool, room under its capacity; for hardware, one board.
- **lease** — slots held for a holder for a while: an id, the pool, the count, who holds it, and when it ends. A lease is what `hull reserve` gives and `hull release` ends; a run either brings one or takes one for its own length.
- **queue** — the runs and reservations waiting for slots, first come first served, each told its place.

**Where pools are declared.** A pool is the host's, or the user's, and not a machine's, so it is declared beside the user's policy, `~/.config/hull/pools.conf`, in hull's s-expressions (§4.1):

```
(pools
  (ci     (capacity 4) (backend vm) (memory "16G"))
  (boards (members (board "rpi4-a" (engine hw) (address "192.168.7.21") (power "pdu:7/1")
                         (console "/dev/ttyUSB0"))
                   (board "rpi4-b" (engine hw) (address "192.168.7.22") (power "pdu:7/2")
                         (console "/dev/ttyUSB1")))))
```

A software pool's capacity is a count of machines, and optionally a budget the core holds runs to, `memory` and `cpus` summed over the pool's machines from their plans, so a pool of vm machines refuses the one whose memory would take it past the host's. A hardware pool's capacity is its members. A machine names its pool in the spec, `(pool "ci")`, a J.1 key, and a run with no pool is in the user's default pool, unlimited unless the user says otherwise, so nothing that runs today waits tomorrow.

**What the core does.** The per-user core (§4.10) already owns every machine its user runs, so admission is its: a run whose pool has no free slot waits in the queue, saying on stderr that it waits, its place and what for, and again each minute, unless `--wait D` bounds it or `--no-wait` refuses at once. A machine's slot is freed when it exits, or when the lease it was run under ends. Leases are kept beside kept machines, under `hull/leases/`, so a core that dies and the next one that starts agree on who holds what; a lease that ends while its machine runs stops the machine, as `hull stop` would, and says why.

**The interface, a runner's:**
- `hull pool ls` — each pool, its capacity, its slots free, held and waited for, and each board's state: up, down, in use, broken.
- `hull reserve POOL [--count N] [--for 30m] [--wait D]` — a lease, its id printed: the slots are the holder's until it ends or is released.
- `hull run --lease ID` — a run in a lease's slot, and `hull run` with no lease takes one for its own length.
- `hull release ID` — the slots back; the lease's machines stopped first.
- `hull lease ls` — the leases, their holders and when they end.

**Scripted use, the holder's condition, 2026-10-22: "this kind of software is used scripted half the time, jenkins servers, atlassian etc., so we can't have something with spurious or unexpected behaviour that ends up being invisible or too annoying."** So pools, and root mode below and in §7, keep these rules, and a design that breaks one is wrong rather than strict:
- **Nothing prompts, and nothing needs a terminal.** Every choice is a flag, a file or the plan; a run under Jenkins behaves as the same run at a desk, but for colour.
- **Every refusal and every wait has its own exit status**, so a pipeline can tell them apart from its workload's: no slot within `--wait`, 75, as sysexits' EX_TEMPFAIL; refused by policy, 77, EX_NOPERM; a lease ended under the run, 79; and the run's own statuses as they are today. The statuses are fixed once chosen, being an interface.
- **What waits says so, where a log will show it.** A wait is said on stderr when it begins and at an interval, never only in `hull pool ls`; a job that sits is never a job that looks hung.
- **Nothing ends that its caller did not give an end.** A run takes a slot for its own length and keeps it until it exits; only a lease from `hull reserve` has a time, which its holder gave, and its end is said in the run's output and in its status, 79. No default timeout stops a long job.
- **Output a script reads is stable.** `hull pool ls` and `hull lease ls` print one line a record, their columns fixed, and `--json` the same as JSON; `hull reserve` prints the lease id alone on stdout, so `lease=$(hull reserve ci)` works.
- **No hidden state.** Pools are a file the user can read, leases a directory, and no environment variable changes what a run does but those documented beside it.

**Hardware is an engine, class `hw`.** §5.0's engines are plugins, and a board is one more: its machine is the board, deployed to and run on, its operations through hooks the pool's member names:
- **power** — on, off and cycle, through a PDU, a relay or a command;
- **console** — a serial device, or a command that gives one, read as the machine's log before the agent is up;
- **deploy** — a dir source rsync'd to the board, as §3.8's deployments are, or an image flashed, or a netboot served;
- **the agent** — the same guest agent, reached over ssh to the board rather than over vsock, the board's sshd and the user's keys doing the authenticating (§4.10's deferral of a network protocol of hull's own), or over the console where there is no network;
- **health** — a check before a board is leased, and a recovery after a run: power cycle, then redeploy, then the board marked broken and out of the pool, said in `hull pool ls`.

Its capability declaration is honest about a board: no overlay unless deploy can lay one, no snapshot, `(network none)` and `ports` as the board's own network allows, the arch the board's, and a device passthrough native, which is what a bench is for. A plan that asks a board for what it cannot do is rejected at plan time, as every engine's is (§6).

**The decision that is the holder's: who enforces a limit across users.** A per-user core can hold only its own user's machines to a pool. A pool shared by a team, or a host-wide limit, needs one owner for the queue and the leases:
- **per-user only** — the default, and enough for one person's CI host;
- **a broker owned by a group** — a small service, `hull-broker`, run as an unprivileged user, its socket owned by a group, `hull-pool`, whose members' cores ask it for slots; no root, at the cost of a resident service the user did not start;
- **a root service** — rejected by §7 unless nothing else can, which nothing above needs.

**Built in this order, each its own piece:** pools with a capacity and a queue in the per-user core, for software machines; leases and the runner's commands; the `hw` engine, against a board the holder names; the broker, once the holder has chosen how it runs.

**Open, and the holder's:** the broker above; whether a software pool's budget counts a plan's `memory` as declared or as measured; whether a lease that ends stops its machine or only refuses the next run; how a board's power and console are named, `pdu:7/1` above being a placeholder; and the names `pool`, `slot` and `lease` themselves, which become a J key, CLI words and a directory, and cannot easily be renamed later. Prior art: LAVA, Linaro's hardware test lab, for boards, health checks and queues; GitHub's and GitLab's runners for leases by label; Kubernetes' resource quotas for budgets.

## 5. Engines

### 5.0 Engines are plugins — the backend set is open

The driver interface is not internal plumbing; it is Hull's **stable plugin API**: `plan(spec) → Plan`, `create/start/stop/destroy`, `exec`, `logs`, `port_map`, `stats`, plus a **capability declaration** — the engine's column of the §6 matrix, published by the engine itself rather than hardcoded in Hull. Everything the CLI exposes goes through this interface, which is what makes backends honestly interchangeable, and (per the project guidelines) it is a natural fit for a C ABI: an engine is a shared object or external helper binary implementing the interface.

**Classes and engines.** ns, vm, and emu are engine *classes* — they define the rows of the capability matrix, the ingress contract, and the migration analysis. Concrete **engines** implement classes: the docker adapter implements ns; the qemu engine implements both vm (KVM acceleration) and emu (TCG); the virtualbox and vmware adapters implement vm. `(backend …)` accepts either a class (host's default engine of that class) or an engine by name. Class-level guarantees are what the conformance suite tests; engine-specific deltas overlay the class column in the matrix.

**First wave — adapter engines** (wrap what users already have installed; maximum reach, minimum novelty):

- **docker** — drives the Docker daemon via its API: spec → container config, `ports`/`volumes`/`env` map directly, `hull exec/logs` ride Docker's own exec/log streams. Honest impedance, declared in its matrix column: dir sources with `(mutable direct)` are *rejected* (Docker can't run an arbitrary rootfs directory in place; `overlay` mode works via a cached `docker import` of the tree), and the adapter **inherits dockerd's root daemon and security posture** — the rootless goal (§7) is delivered by the native ns engine in wave two, not by this adapter. A podman variant is nearly free (same API surface, plus `--rootfs` makes `direct` dir sources native and restores rootless).
- **qemu** — the reference engine, no daemon to adapt to: implements vm (accel=kvm) and emu (accel=tcg) with everything §5.2–5.3 describe (virtiofs, vsock agent, micro/full profiles).
- **virtualbox** — full-profile vm-class via `VBoxManage`: block projection (§3.9) for the rootfs, vboxsf for volumes, NAT rules for ports, agent via seed ISO.
- **vmware** — full-profile vm-class for Workstation/Fusion via `vmrun`/VIX (ESXi/vCenter is enterprise-tier territory, Appendix I): generated `.vmx` + VMDK via block projection, HGFS shared folders for volumes, agent via seed ISO. Live migration on desktop VMware: none (vMotion is vCenter's, and that's fine — it maps to the paid tier anyway).

**Second wave — native engines:** the crun-based rootless ns engine and the microVM profile with bundled kernel (§5.2, Appendix F) — these deliver the security-model promises the adapters can't, and slot in without any spec change precisely because classes, not engines, define the contract.

**Reserved pigeonholes:** the abstraction target is deliberately broader than "a Linux machine" — it is *an isolated execution environment over a directory of files*. Declared engine-name slots are reserved (name, class, expected capability shape) even before any implementation exists, so specs and docs can reference them stably:

- **hyperv** — vm-class, the **Windows-host slot**: driven via PowerShell/WMI, rootfs via VHDX block projection (§3.9/§3.13), volumes via SMB/Plan-9 shares, and the agent control plane over **Hyper-V sockets** (AF_HYPERV — Hyper-V's native vsock equivalent, so Appendix B ports with a transport swap, not a redesign). Reserved now so Windows host support lands as an engine, not a rewrite. (Distinct from the "Windows containers" non-goal: this is Windows *hosting* Hull machines.)

That admits further engines like:

- **chroot** — near-trivial, useful as the reference plugin implementation;
- **DOSBox** — `(source "dir:./c")` mounts as `C:`, `command` becomes the autoexec line, extra `volumes` become additional DOS mounts, `(media …)` cdrom entries appear as disc drives reconstructed from file collections (§3.10, copy protection included), and nearly everything else (`ports`, `memory` semantics, `ssh`) is declared `rejected` in its capability table — which is fine, because the matrix makes partial engines *safe* rather than broken;
- **Wine**, **user-mode Linux**, **wasm runtimes** — same story with different capability subsets.

An engine declares what it can do; the plan step enforces it; the conformance suite is tagged per capability, so any third-party engine can run exactly the subset of the shared tests its declaration claims — and gets held to it. Spec fields an engine doesn't know are `rejected` by default, never ignored. The result: the same `hull.conf` + files-in-a-directory workflow drives any execution substrate anyone cares to plug in, and "switch backend" stays a one-line change all the way from crun to DOSBox.

### 5.1 The `ns` class — namespaces (default)

Wave one implements this class with the **docker adapter** (§5.0). The wave-two **native engine**: crun under user namespaces; idmapped mounts for volume ownership sanity; cgroups v2 limits via systemd user-session delegation; pasta for networking. Startup in tens of milliseconds. Shared host kernel — the fast path for trusted dev work, and the engine that delivers the rootless promise natively.

### 5.2 The `vm` class — hardware-virtualized (reference engine: qemu)

The default `micro` profile:

- QEMU `-M microvm` (or cloud-hypervisor) with a **bundled minimal guest kernel** (virtio, virtiofs, vsock, overlayfs; built from a pinned LTS config, updated with Hull releases). Boot target: 100–300 ms.
- Rootfs: the composefs image — or the dir source — over virtiofs (plus `upper/` over a second share when `(mutable overlay)`), assembled by a ~200 KB static **guest agent** that is PID 1, mounts the root, applies the spec (env, workdir, uid), and exec's the workload.
- The agent speaks a small protocol over **vsock**: exec, logs/stdio streaming, stats, graceful stop. This is what makes `hull exec`/`hull logs` byte-for-byte identical UX across backends.
- Ports: host-side forwarders mapped from the same `ports` spec onto the machine's stable ingress address (§8.1).
- `(kernel image)` lets an image ship its own kernel at a well-known path for kernel-dependent workloads.

The `full` profile boots a standard QEMU machine type instead — full distro init, custom kernels/modules, PCI/USB passthrough, optional GUI. Startup in seconds; the matrix makes the cost visible. The **virtualbox** and **vmware** engines (§5.0) implement this class in full-profile form only, via the block-projection path (§3.9) — same class contract, their own matrix deltas. If VirtualBox's API drag ever exceeds its value it is the first engine to cut — the spec doesn't change either way.

### 5.3 The `emu` class — software emulation (qemu, accel=tcg)

The same driver as `vm` with hardware acceleration removed — same profiles, same kernel strategy, same virtiofs shares, same guest agent, same vsock control plane (with a virtio-serial fallback where vhost-vsock isn't available). Two reasons it exists:

- **No KVM:** locked-down hosts, nested environments, CI runners without /dev/kvm. The spec runs unchanged, just slower.
- **Foreign architecture:** set `(arch arm64)` (or riscv64, …) and the machine boots a foreign-arch kernel and rootfs on any host. Combined with dir sources this means: put an ARM rootfs in a directory, run it on your x86 laptop, switch it to `(backend vm)` unchanged when you're on ARM hardware.

Cost is honesty-first: TCG runs roughly 5–20× slower than native and the matrix says so. A middle path for foreign-arch *processes* (not kernels) is `ns` with `(binfmt true)` — qemu-user under binfmt_misc, faster than full-system emulation, flagged `emulated` in the matrix because syscall emulation has edge cases.

---

## 6. Capability matrix (deep dive)

The matrix is data, not documentation: assembled at load time from each engine's capability declaration (§5.0 — core engines ship theirs built in, plugins publish their own), consulted at plan time, and printable via `hull plan --explain`. Every spec field maps, per backend, to one of:

- **native** — honored exactly;
- **emulated(cost)** — honored via a documented mechanism with a stated cost (e.g. vboxsf volume = slow I/O);
- **rejected(reason)** — plan fails with the reason and, where one exists, the suggested alternative.

| Spec field / feature | ns | vm | emu |
|---|---|---|---|
| `command`, `env`, `workdir` | native | native (agent) | native (agent) |
| `(source "dir:…")` (+ `(mutable direct)`) | native (idmapped bind) | native under passthrough; block projection rejects unless `writeback` (§3.9) | native under passthrough; same block rule |
| `ports` → stable ingress address (§8.1) | native (pasta) | emulated: host forwarder | emulated: host forwarder |
| `(ssh true)` / `hull ssh` | native | native | native |
| `volumes rw/ro` | native (idmapped bind) | native (virtiofs; vboxsf/HGFS on the virtualbox/vmware engines is slow) | native (virtiofs) |
| `cpus`, `memory` | native (cgroups; memory = ceiling) | native (hard allocation) | native (hard allocation; cpus = threads) |
| `readonly_root` | native | native | native |
| `arch` ≠ host | rejected → `emulated` with `(binfmt true)` (qemu-user) | rejected: KVM is same-arch → use emu | native (that's the point) |
| host networking, `(network host)` | native, **opt-in flag** | rejected: no shared netns → use `ports` | rejected: same |
| no network, `(network none)` | native (an empty netns, lo up) | native (no NIC) | native (no NIC) |
| device passthrough | rejected in rootless (no nodes) | emulated: virtio/VFIO/USB where permitted | emulated: QEMU-emulated devices only |
| custom kernel / modules | rejected: shared host kernel | native (`kernel` key; `full` profile for distro kernels) | native |
| inotify on volumes | native | rejected-by-default: virtiofs event gaps → poll option offered | rejected-by-default: same |
| startup latency (informational) | ~10 ms | ~100–300 ms (micro), seconds (full) | seconds; workload 5–20× slower |
| kernel isolation boundary (informational) | none (namespaces) | hardware VM | full guest stack, software boundary |

Two rules keep this honest:

1. **No silent degradation.** If a field would behave *differently* (not just slower) on the selected backend, that's `rejected` unless the user opts in (`hull run --accept vm:inotify-poll`). Slower-but-identical is `emulated` and merely warned about once.
2. **The matrix is versioned with the binary** and covered by a conformance test suite: every `native`/`emulated` cell has an integration test that runs the same spec on that backend and asserts identical observable behavior (exit codes, file contents, port reachability, env, uid mapping). The matrix isn't a promise — it's a test manifest.

`hull check --backend vm` runs plan-only validation, so switchability can be enforced in CI: a project that must stay backend-portable adds one CI line per backend.

---

## 7. Security model

**Decided 2026-10-15, by the holder: hull does everything it can as the user, and asks for root only at the very end of the line.** In the holder's words, "our basic ideology is that docker is broken precisely because it needs root for everything". So root is never a convenience or a default. Where something can be done as the user, through a user namespace, a user-owned socket or a file the user owns, it is. A design that reaches for root has not yet found the user's way, and the engines that inherit root from what they wrap say so in their declarations. How a spec asks for root at the end of the line is designed below, as root mode, and not built.

**What needs root, and what only seems to — written 2026-10-22, at the holder's asking, from what this tree has met.** Most of what hosts give root for is either a setting an administrator makes once or a habit kept from 2013, and the root that a run itself needs is nearly nothing.
- **What no user namespace can do:** load a module, set a global sysctl or the clock; mount a block filesystem, ext4, xfs or EROFS from a disk or a loop device, the kernel allowing a user namespace only tmpfs, proc, binds, overlayfs since 5.11 and FUSE, since a crafted image would reach every filesystem driver; configure the host's own network, its bridges, NAT and routes, where passt does a machine's in user space instead (§4.11); and make a device node, though passing one through that the user may already open is not root.
- **What one setting, made once by the host's owner, gives every user after:** user namespaces themselves, on by default on Debian and Fedora, restricted per AppArmor profile on Ubuntu 24.04 and switchable off on RHEL, the hurdle that stops everything else; many uids inside a machine, an image's `_apt` or its `postgres`, by ranges in `/etc/subuid` and `/etc/subgid` and shadow's setuid `newuidmap` and `newgidmap`, without which one uid maps and apt's sandbox user fails; a port below 1024, by `net.ipv4.ip_unprivileged_port_start`; ping, by `net.ipv4.ping_group_range`; CPU and memory limits, by cgroup v2 delegated to the user, as systemd's `user@.service` does and a host without systemd, this one, does not, so its `cpus` and `memory` go unenforced; `/dev/kvm`, `/dev/vhost-vsock` and other devices, by a group or a udev rule; and a service account with no login, Jenkins's say, which has no `/run/user/UID` unless lingering is enabled or the tool copes without one.
- **Why test servers run docker as root anyway.** Docker came in 2013, before user namespaces were usable; overlayfs in one came in 2021, cgroup v2 delegation about then, and rootless docker was general only in 20.10, in 2020. CI was built in that gap around one root daemon, its socket mounted into jobs and its `docker` group, which is root by another name, in every install guide. Rootless docker meanwhile asks for a per-user daemon kept alive with lingering, subuid ranges, slirp4netns's slow network, and on older kernels fuse-overlayfs or vfs storage, and it breaks docker-in-docker; and either way the workspace is someone else's afterwards, host root's under a rootful daemon and a subuid's under a rootless one, so the next job's `git clean` fails, and `--privileged` or `sudo` is what gets a job past the error.
- **What hull already removes:** a machine's root is the user, so what it writes in a workspace is the CI user's and cleans up; there is no daemon to install or keep alive, each user's core starting when needed and leaving when idle; each job has its own core and no shared socket or group; and networking and stable addresses need no root.
- **What it still has to remove, in this order:** ~~a core that works with no `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, for a service account~~, done 2026-10-22 (§4.10); ~~`hull doctor`, which checks each setting above and prints, for the host's owner, the exact lines that would unlock what is missing, once~~, done 2026-10-22, below; many uids through `newuidmap` where subuid ranges exist, with an image's owners kept in its manifest, decided by the holder 2026-10-22 and designed below, and the single map, said, where they do not; each known breakage said with its way round, ~~apt's sandbox user under one uid, a port below 1024~~, done 2026-10-23, below, and limits without delegation, which no engine built yet meets, chroot declaring no `cpus` or `memory` at all and a vm's being qemu's own allocation; ~~and packaging that does the one-time parts, an AppArmor profile for Ubuntu's restriction, passt's profile (§4.11), and a declared CI user's subuid ranges~~, done 2026-10-25, below.
- **Two breakages said, with their way round, 2026-10-23.** A chroot machine that would be mapped many uids, above an overlay with no upper kept, and is mapped one because the host gives the user no subordinate range, says so once on the workload's stderr: `hull: one uid is mapped, the host giving this user no subordinate range: a chown to another of the machine's users fails, and apt-get wants -o APT::Sandbox::User=root; hull doctor gives the line that adds a range`. The engine tells the machine by the byte that already says whether it mapped many, `O` beside `M` and `S`; a machine of one uid by design, in place or under a kept upper, says nothing. It is said at run time and not in the plan, since whether `newuidmap` succeeds is known only once the machine's namespace exists. And a vm's host port below `net.ipv4.ip_unprivileged_port_start` is refused, as before, before the machine boots, now as `port 80 on ADDRESS: below 1024, the lowest a user may bind (net.ipv4.ip_unprivileged_port_start): give a host port of 1024 or more, or see hull doctor` rather than `Permission denied`. **Measured** in `test/cli_test.sh`, hull run in a user namespace of the user's own, whose root has no range here, with a core of its own: the line said and the machine run; and on the host, which has a range, not said. And in `test/qemu_test.sh`: a vm of port 80 refused with the line. **Sabotage:** baseline first, in scratch copies, its qemu suite passing whole: the one uid left unsaid, the line said where many are mapped, and the low port refused as before with `Permission denied`, each failed a named case.
- **Many uids, with an image's owners kept — decided 2026-10-22 by the holder, over accepting chown and keeping none, and over both in turn.** What forced it: a postinst giving a file to its package's own system user fails under one mapped uid, `chown: changing ownership of '/run/dnsmasq': Invalid argument`, measured in a `hull build` step on `debian:13` and on `trixie-slim` (netcfgd's base), so `dnsmasq-base`, and the like of every network package, cannot be built. The shape, which keeps the files on the user's disk the user's, so that nothing is left that the user needs a namespace to delete, the trap rootless podman's `podman unshare rm` exists for:
  - **chroot machines and build steps map the user's subordinate range** through `newuidmap` and `newgidmap` where `/etc/subuid` and `/etc/subgid` give one, 0 the user and 1 on the range, so a chown inside a machine to an id the range holds works. Where there is none, one uid as now, which the plan says, and `hull doctor` gives the line that adds a range.
  - **A write with an owner other than the user lands only in an overlay's upper layer**, a tmpfs in the machine's own namespace, never in the user's tree. So a build step runs on an overlay of its parent rather than directly on a checkout, and its commit walks the merged tree from inside that namespace, recording each owner as the machine saw it, as A.3's explicit `(owner (uid n) (gid n))` where it is neither root nor the user.
  - **A machine of a stored tree gets its owners back** when it starts: what the manifest gives to an explicit owner is chowned in the merged tree, which copies only that file up into the tmpfs. A `(mutable direct)` machine, writing the user's own tree in place, keeps one uid, since owners there would be owners on the user's disk.
  - **Built so far, 2026-10-23: the map.** A chroot machine above an overlay, whose root is not read-only, is mapped from outside by `newuidmap` and `newgidmap`, 0 the user and 1 on the range, the engine telling the machine by a pipe whether it did, so that it maps its one uid itself where not; the user's own groups, unmapped there and so nobody's, are dropped. **Measured** in `test/cli_test.sh`, on a host with a range: a chown to 101 in an overlay machine works and the file is 101's there, and one in a directory used in place is refused, one uid as before; and by hand, `dnsmasq-base` installed in an overlay machine of `debian:13` with exit 0 and `/run/dnsmasq` its `dnsmasq` user's, apt run with no `APT::Sandbox::User` given, though its download sandbox fell back to root until the materialized tree root stopped being 0700 (§110, and the step record below). Build steps, which run directly on a checkout, keep one uid until they move onto an overlay, the next step.
  - **How a build step keeps owners, decided 2026-10-23 by the holder: strictly, never on disk,** over running it in place under the store's `tmp/` with many uids and removing the tree through a mapped namespace, which is smaller but leaves files plain `rm` cannot clear if hull dies mid-step. The mechanism, as designed, in the order it is built:
    1. **The machine's tree, out as a tar, `(export PATH)`** — built 2026-10-23, on chroot, with `engine/tree.c` given a one-mount mode (by statx's mount id, a bind from the same filesystem keeping its device) and xattrs, `security.capability` among them, as a directory's commit keeps them: once the workload has ended, a chroot machine's init, still in its namespaces, writes its merged root as `engine/tree.c`'s tar to a file it opened before the pivot, and so the user's, each entry with its owner as the machine saw it, staying on the root's own filesystem, so no `/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev`, volume or secret is in it. Only the tar's bytes are on the user's disk.
       The init opens the file before the pivot, close-on-exec, and writes the tar once the workload has ended and its output is read, before it says the end. Only an overlay machine whose root is not read-only takes one, since in place the tree is the user's own directory already. A failed export empties the file, which no commit takes, says why on the workload's stderr, and makes the end 125. **Measured** in `test/cli_test.sh`: an overlay machine giving a file to 101 exports it as 101's, the probe as root's and `/proc` as an empty directory, and the tar commits with the owner kept; one in place is refused. **Sabotage:** baseline first, in scratch copies: the tar never written, every mount written into it, and an export allowed in place, each failed a named case. A failed export said and made 125 has no case, since nothing a test can put in a user namespace's tree, a device node, would fail one.
    2. **A commit from a tar, owners kept** — built 2026-10-23, as `hull_store_commit_tar` and `hull commit --tar FILE REF`, `-` for stdin: the store reads such a tar into objects and a manifest, uid 0 as `(owner root)` and any other as A.3's explicit ids, the build's discard paths and `/tmp` dropped as a commit of a directory drops them.
       It reads what `engine/tree.c` writes, ustar with pax records for a long path or link, a large id or size, and `SCHILY.xattr.*`, into the same items a directory's commit walks to, so one tail makes the manifest for both. An object is read to its entry's size and no further, and a tar that ends before its two blocks of zeroes, or whose pax header cannot be read, is refused and names nothing. It is a door of its own as well: `docker export … | hull commit --tar - app/1` keeps the owners and setuid bits that unpacking the export as a user loses, which netcfgd measured on `trixie-slim`, eight setuid-root programs kept by `hull materialize` and none by the export. **Measured** in `test/store_test.sh`, on a tar Python's `tarfile` makes: a directory of root's and one of 101's, a file of 101's and 102's with a `user.` xattr, a symlink, and a name of 124 bytes, each back in the manifest as it was; and a tar cut short refused, with no ref made. **Sabotage:** baseline first, in scratch copies: owners lost, the pax path ignored, xattrs dropped, an object read past its size, a cut tar accepted, and a walk that failed named anyway, each failed a named case, six of six. A cut tar accepted survived the first run, the test's cut falling in a pax header, which is refused before the end is looked for; a tar whole to its last entry but with no blocks of zeroes after it is what holds it.
    3. **Owners given back, `(owners FILE)`** — built 2026-10-23, on chroot, above an overlay, its list each entry's uid, gid and path each ended by a NUL, since a path may hold a newline: before the workload, the init gives each path the list names its owner in the merged tree, which copies only that file's metadata up into the tmpfs. A stored tree's checkout is all the user's, so without it a second step would lose what the first gave other owners, and a machine of a stored tree would run with none. The qemu engine gives them back the same way since 2026-10-25, in the overlay it mounts host-side, the range mapped into its namespace from outside by `newuidmap` rather than from a chroot's own clone; the helpers are shared (`engine/idmap.c`, §4.11). And `hull run` of a stored image, not only a build step, emits the list: `store_sources` reads the manifest's owners into `(owners …)` beside the checkout's dir source, so an image started by `hull run` carries its owners on either engine — the step path had been the only one that did, which left a stored image flattened to root until 2026-10-25 (§4.11).
       The init reads the list after the pivot and before the workload, and gives each path its owner, not following a link; those that cannot be given, as where one uid is mapped, are counted and said once on the workload's stderr. **The cost, measured:** overlayfs copies a file up with the directories above it, and cannot copy up one whose group the namespace does not map, `EOVERFLOW`, so a file under a directory setgid to an unmapped group, as this build tree's are to `users`, cannot be given an owner; a stored checkout in a home directory is the user's group, which is. **Measured** in `test/cli_test.sh`: a file of the user's on disk listed as 101's and 102's is that in the machine and still the user's on disk; in place the key is refused. **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies: owners never given and the key allowed in place each failed a named case; a refusal left unsaid survived, this host having a range and so refusing none.
    4. **A step, then** — built 2026-10-23, so all four are, and a step's owners are kept: above an overlay of its parent's private copy, its copies put in, its parent's owners given back, many uids, its tree exported when it ends, and that tar committed. **Sabotage:** baseline first, in scratch copies: never mapping many, and mapping them while telling the machine to map its own, each failed a named case. Mapping many under a directory used in place was not run: its chown would give a file on the scratch disk to a subordinate id, which nothing but a namespace could then delete, the very cost the shape is for avoiding.
       A step with a run line, on chroot, runs `(mutable overlay)` over its parent's private copy with `(owners …)` from the parent's manifest, through `hull_store_owners`, and `(export …)`, and its tar is committed with `/tmp/` and its discard paths as the drop list; a step of copies alone, or on another backend, commits its directory as before. The step's scratch is made not setgid, though the store's directory may be, so what is made in it takes the user's own group, which the namespace maps; this build tree's are setgid to `users`, which it does not, and the first run met `EOVERFLOW` there. **Measured** in `test/build_test.sh`, on a host with a range: a step gives a file to 101, the next step sees it as 101's, and the last manifest keeps it; and by hand, `dnsmasq-base` installed by a `hull build` step on `debian:13` with exit 0 and no `APT::Sandbox::User`, `run/dnsmasq` kept as 999's and apt's `partial` as `_apt`'s, 42, where the evening before the same step failed in its postinst. **netcfgd installed its whole hwsim set in one step on `trixie-slim` 2026-10-25** — `build-essential kmod iw wpasupplicant iproute2 dnsmasq-base dhcpcd-base iputils-ping`, exit 0, every binary its bake checks present — and the manifest kept eight setuid files and the non-root owners 42, 999 and 65534, which `docker export` through `tar -x` as a user cannot. **Until 2026-10-25 apt's download sandbox did not engage, and the earlier "sandbox user working" above was an inference from exit 0 and the kept ownership, not a reading:** apt warned `_apt` cannot access `/var/cache/apt/archives/partial` (`Permission denied`) and downloaded as root instead, the install unsandboxed. My first guess, that it was apt's own rootless behaviour over a root-created `partial`, was wrong, and netcfgd disproved it: ownership was not the cause, and no non-root uid could execute anything in the step, `EACCES` on the `execve` of a 0755 binary. The cause was the materialized tree's `/` left 0700 (§110), untraversable by the mapped `_apt`; fixed there, the root now 0755. **netcfgd measured the fix end to end on a `b7126fe` build, 2026-10-25, and the root mode was the whole of it:** apt's sandbox fully engages, `su -s /bin/sh _apt` runs, `apt-get update` and the hwsim set give zero unsandboxed warnings, and `_apt` owns its own `archives/partial` and `lists/partial` at `0700 42:0` after. And **`systemd-sysv` installs in a step** — the set netcfgd's sandbox rig baked with docker precisely because a one-uid step could not give files to a package's own system user — `systemd systemd-sysv udev dbus util-linux iproute2` exit 0, `systemd-network` 998 and `messagebus` 997 created and owning `/var/lib/systemd/network` and `/run/systemd/netif`, the manifest keeping four of their entries, `_apt`'s three, and eleven setuid/setgid. Its method defeats the step cache, which would otherwise replay a cached result: each step opens by printing `stat /`, so the `0755` shows from inside. The half that decides netcfgd's move off docker is that an image built this way *boots*, and **netcfgd reported it tested and good 2026-10-25:** its four rigs moved from docker-exported `dir:` trees to `hull build` images, all four images build, and three rigs pass from stored sources — natcap 37/37, the sandbox rig 31/31 with **systemd as PID 1**, and OpenRC 13/13 under `/sbin/openrc-init` with the service starting and stopping. So the boot half this entry recorded as untested is netcfgd's, measured. Its tier-wide migration is its and its holder's. The first run kept no owner past the first step: the owners' list read each id from the owner record's first sibling rather than the record, and gave every id as 0. **`hull push`'s layer writer read owners the same way**, `hull_store_layer`, so a pushed layer gave every explicit owner as root: found here, and fixed the same day, `test/pull_test.sh` now pushing a tree with a file of 101's and 102's and reading the layer apart from hull, which the old reading fails. **Sabotage:** baseline first, in scratch copies: never exporting, the owners not listed, the owner record misread, discards kept and `/tmp` kept each failed a named case, and so did the scratch left setgid, once the suite made its own store's directory setgid to one of the user's other groups: the first run survived it, the copies being under `/tmp`, which is not setgid, so the case had been left to where the tree happened to sit.
  - **`hull pull` keeps a layer's owners, since 2026-10-23,** with `hull push` keeping them on the way out, which it had not: the unpacker reads each entry's uid and gid, from ustar or pax, and keeps the last layer's owner of each path where it is not root's, and the commit gives a path in that table its ids over the disk's, all the user's. A whiteout leaves its row behind, which no path of the tree then matches. Asked for by netcfgd, whose images keep setuid bits and owners only by a store that keeps them both ways. **Measured** in `test/pull_test.sh`: a tree with a file of 101's and 102's, committed from a tar, pushed, read apart from hull as theirs in the layer, and pulled back as the same tree but for its provenance and the runtime a pull gives. **Sabotage:** baseline first, in scratch copies: the owners never taken from the unpacker, a header's uid unread, and the commit ignoring them, each failed the pull-back case.
  - **Not decided:** docker, whose containers are its daemon's.
- **Loop mounts, which build systems reach for, have three ways round, none of them root:** an image made from a directory without mounting it, `mkfs.ext4 -d`, `mkfs.erofs`, `mksquashfs`, `mkfs.fat` with `mcopy`, `xorriso`, `systemd-repart --offline` or `genimage`, which is how §3.9's block projection is meant to make one; an existing image read or changed through FUSE, `fuse2fs`, `squashfuse`, `erofsfuse` or `fusefat`, given `/dev/fuse`; and, where a tool insists on the kernel's own mount, a build step on the vm class, whose guest root loop-mounts as it likes while the host's user needs only `/dev/kvm`. `hull doctor` checks `/dev/fuse` and `/dev/kvm` for these.
- **`hull doctor`, since 2026-10-22,** `cli/doctor.c`, reads what the host gives this user and changes nothing, user namespaces being tried in a child that exits. A line a check, its status the first word for a script to read, `ok`, `missing`, `limited` or `absent`, and under what is missing or limited the one-time line for the host's owner, `  fix (as root): …`: user namespaces, and for each of the three settings that switch them off the one to change; subuid and subgid ranges with a setuid or capable `newuidmap` and `newgidmap`; overlay in a user namespace by the kernel's version; cgroup v2 delegated, `cpu` and `memory` enabled in the user's own cgroup and its `subtree_control` the user's to write; the unprivileged port start and ping's group range; `/dev/kvm`, `/dev/vhost-vsock` and `/dev/fuse`; the runtime directory chosen and whether it is in memory; and passt, qemu, docker and the image tools, looked for on PATH and then in the sbin directories a user's PATH leaves out, as Debian's has `mkfs.ext4` in `/usr/sbin`, and said where found. Its status is 1 only where what everything needs cannot be had, user namespaces or a runtime directory, and 0 otherwise, so a CI job can run it first without a merely limited host failing the job. The cli suite takes user namespaces away by a namespace's own `user.max_user_namespaces=0`, which leaves the host's alone, and lays out each runtime choice in a namespace whose `/run/user`, `/dev/shm` and `/tmp` are its own, an overlay standing for a directory on a disk since it is no tmpfs whatever is under it. **Sabotage,** baseline first, in scratch copies: breaking each of these failed a named case, nine of nine: user namespaces judged by the fork alone, the sysctl's fix unsaid, either blocker not blocking, the runtime directory not judged for memory, the frontend's choice not applied, an empty `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` taken for one, tools never found, and a status word changed.
- **Packaging the one-time parts, 2026-10-25 — an AppArmor profile, and the other two already met.** The last item of the list above had three parts. The one that needed building is **an AppArmor profile for Ubuntu's restriction** (`packaging/apparmor/hull.in`, `make install-apparmor`): Ubuntu 24.04 denies unprivileged user namespaces unless the process is under a profile that grants them, which the chroot and qemu engines need, so the profile grants `userns` to the hull binary and confines nothing else — the restriction stays on for the rest of the host, where doctor's other line, `sysctl …=0`, turns it off for everything. It attaches by the binary's path, and hull runs from where it is built rather than a fixed location, so the target fills the path in from `HULL_BIN` (the built binary by default). doctor's user-namespace fix now names `make install-apparmor` (`cli/doctor.c`). **Verified by hand:** `make install-apparmor DESTDIR=…` writes `/etc/apparmor.d/hull` with the path substituted, and `apparmor_parser -Q --skip-cache` (4.1.0, abi 4.0, no load so no root) parses it; the check discriminates, the raw template with `@HULL@` unfilled and a profile with `userns` misspelt each failing to parse. **Its functional unblock on Ubuntu 24.04 is for an adopter there to confirm,** as this host is Debian, which does not restrict user namespaces and so cannot exercise the profile — the kernel-path lesson, that what cannot be tested here is said to be unconfirmed. The other two parts were already met and needed no file: **passt's profile is moot** since the pid-file removal (§4.11, 2026-10-21) — the engine opens `passt.log` itself and `dup2`s it onto passt's stdout and stderr, so passt opens nothing under `/run/user` for Debian's passt profile to refuse, the pid file having been the only thing it did; and **a CI user's subuid ranges** are the `usermod --add-subuids 100000-165535 --add-subgids …` line `hull doctor` already prints, which a CI host's owner runs once for the agent's account. No `.deb` is built, matching `install-nss`: the artifacts install by name into system directories, the one root step each, and nothing else needs root.
- **Limits without systemd need more than a cgroup the user owns,** found writing doctor's fix line, which first said to make one and chown it. A process moves into a cgroup only by one who may write the `cgroup.procs` of the lowest cgroup holding both where it is and where it goes, and that is the root of the tree, so the user's processes have to start in the delegated cgroup, as a login or a service does, and `/sys/fs/cgroup` is made anew at each boot. This host, sysvinit with elogind, puts a session in `/sys/fs/cgroup/c1`, root's. Systemd's `user@.service` delegates at login; without it, LXC's `pam_cgfs` (`libpam-cgfs`, in Debian 13) does for a PAM session, ssh's included, which is how a CI agent over ssh arrives; and a service with no login has its init script make the cgroup, chown it and start the service in it. libcgroup's `cgrulesengd` (`cgroup-tools`) moves processes by uid too, but after they start, from a root daemon that must stay up. **`pam_cgfs` is named from its description and not yet tried here**, whether it delegates on this host's mixed v1 and v2 hierarchy being unmeasured: that needs root, and is the holder's to try. A vm's `cpus` and `memory` hold anyway, through qemu's own `-smp` and `-m`.

**Root mode — design, not yet built, asked for by the holder 2026-10-22: "we will eventually also want the dangerous full root mode like docker has. But we want to go to maximum lengths to ensure that users don't use it unless they really really have to."** It is the end of the line the decision above names, and it is designed so that reaching it is never the easy path:
- **It is not a key.** No `--privileged`, no `(root true)`, no setting that makes it a default. A machine asks for what it needs, a device, a capability, a mount, and the planner offers root only for a field every rootless engine on the host has rejected, naming each field and why the rootless engines refused it. A machine whose fields a rootless engine satisfies is never given root, whatever it asks.
- **It is not installed.** Root mode is a separate helper, `hull-root`, which `make install-root` installs as root and nothing else does; a host without it has no root mode at all, and `hull engines` says so. The helper is the smallest thing that can do the root part, starting the machine's processes with what the plan was granted and nothing more, not a daemon that runs machines.
- **It is the host owner's, then the user's, then each run's.** The host's root lists in `/etc/hull/root.conf` which users may ask, for which machines by their plan's hash, and for which fields; a user not listed is refused. The user, then, accepts it on each run by the plan's hash, `hull run --accept root:SHA256`, never by a flag that outlives the run: a changed plan is a new hash, and must be accepted again. There is no environment variable and no user config that accepts it.
- **It is loud, and it is never surprising.** A root machine is marked ROOT in `hull ps`, at the head of its log and in the plan's report; every start, exec and stop of one is written to the host's audit log, `/var/log/hull/root.log`, with the user and the plan's hash, and a run whose audit line cannot be written is refused rather than run unrecorded. It ends with its run, as any machine does: a root machine left running by accident is prevented by `(on_disconnect stop)`, its default, not by a timer that would stop a long job without its asking (§4.12's scripted-use rules).
- **It is scriptable without being easy.** A pipeline carries the acceptance as a value, `hull plan --root-hash` printing the hash to accept, so a CI job runs root mode the same each time and a changed plan fails loudly, status 77, with the new hash and the line of `/etc/hull/root.conf` it would need, rather than being accepted by an old token. Nothing about it prompts.
- **It is offered with its alternatives.** Before root, the planner says what would do instead: the vm class for a kernel boundary or modules, a `(security (allow …))` capability in a user namespace, a device passed to a vm, a volume instead of a mount. A user reaches root only having been told what else would serve.

**Tried as designed, the holder's word, 2026-10-22: "maybe that is too extreme, but let's try it for now and see if it flies"**, under the scripted-use rules of §4.12; what proves too much in use is to be cut back, not worked around. **Open, and the holder's:** whether the helper is setuid, a root service the host starts, or both; what fields root mode grants at all, a device node, a capability outside the user namespace, a host mount, the host's network; and whether an engine that already rides a root daemon, docker, counts as root mode, which today it is not called, though its declaration says what it inherits.

- No root daemon, no setuid helpers, no privileged socket. The resident component is the per-user core (§2), an unprivileged service owned by the invoking user; the attack surface of "Hull installed" is user-owned processes and a user-owned socket. Adapter engines inherit the wrapped runtime's posture — the docker adapter rides dockerd's root daemon, and its matrix column declares it — while native engines carry the full rootless model.
- ns backend: user namespaces map root-in-container to an unprivileged subordinate uid; seccomp default profile (crun's) + `no_new_privs` on by default; capabilities are empty-by-default and additive via `(security (allow …))` — there is deliberately **no `--privileged`**. Workloads that need it are told to use the vm backend, which is the correct tool.
- vm/emu backends: the kernel boundary is the headline feature. The guest agent treats the vsock channel as the only control plane; no host filesystem is visible except declared volumes.
